Ultraviolet finite non-local Hamiltonian perturbation Quantum Field Theories and their weak adiabatic limit
Aleksei Bykov

Abstract
The non-local quantum field theories attract interest in the mathematical and physical community as candidates for effective description of the reality taking into account the quantum gravity effects. The standard methods, developed for the conventional local quantum field theories, often are not applicable. In this paper the Hamiltonian approach, originally proposed for quantization of the non-local effective theory inspired by non-commutative geometry of the Doplicher-Fredenhagen-Roberts Quantum Spacetime, is generalized to a large class of ultraviolet finite non-local Hamiltonian perturbative Quantum Field Theories. General properties of such theories are discussed. The weak adiabatic limit existence is proved for the whole class of theories.



1
Introduction

Local quantum field theory is a powerful framework to build models of physical reality. In particular, the Standard Model is known for its extremely precise predictions. Its modifications, known as “Beyond Standard Model Physics”, are also local quantum field theories. For the models of quantum gravity, local quantum field theories serve as low-energy effective theories.
Despite this success, it is very unlikely that a physical theory including all known interactions can be built within that framework. In particular, it was argued by many authors, starting from the early days of the quantum field theory
[
5
,
6
]
, that quantum gravity can not be local. The physical reason of that is the interplay between Heisenberg uncertainty relations and the role played by the energy and momentum in General Relativity. As a result, any attempt of a too much localized measurement inevitably produces a dramatic change of the geometry such as a black hole formation, making the measurement impossible. Assuming that the main object of the theory is a system of (in principle) measurable observables, we conclude that the language of local QFT is not appropriate. There is a related long-standing conjecture that the gravity-induced non-locality may be a cure of the ultra-violet divergences of local QFT
[
7
]
, since the later are caused by the singular products of point-localised observables, or, equivalently, by point-localized elementary scatterings.

For this reason, non-local quantum field theories attract interest. In particular, a lot of non-local models are considered as effective theories of quantum field theory on quantum (non-commutative) spacetimes, e.g.
[
3
,
4
]
.

Remark 1.1
.

Locality plays a crucial role in both the mathematical and physical aspects of Quantum Field Theory. The meaning of this word is, however, slightly different. In mathematics, in particular in algebraic Quantum Field Theory, by locality one usually means commutativity of the observables related to measurements performed in space-like separated regions of spacetime. In physics one is usually concerned with the locality of the interactions. The precise meaning of this varies from approach to approach, but one expects that the terms describing the interaction (e.g. the higher than quadratic part of the Hamiltonian or Lagrangian) are local non-linear functionals of quantum fields. In the language of Feynman graphs, it may be interpreted as the localization of all “elementary scatterings” at points of spacetime. These two meanings are not unrelated. For example, in causal perturbation theory the “physical” locality together with causality implies the “algebraic one”
[
19
]
. Throughout this paper we by default understand non-locality in the “physical” sense.

The main problem of this area of mathematical physics is the lack of a universal understanding of what a non-local quantum field theory is. The ambiguities arise even at the perturbation theory level.
In conventional quantum field theory, there are different approaches, involving various mathematical constructions but describing essentially the same physics. This equivalence is lost in the absence of locality, as well as the applicability of the most popular methods just can not be used anymore. For example, the approaches based on the functional integrals, widely used in physics, fail to provide a unitary theory in the non-local case
[
4
]
1
1

1

In this paper we consider the physical Lorentzian signature of the spacetime metrics only. In the Euclidean signature, for which the functional integral approach, as usual, is much more reasonable and is widely studied, e.g.
[
20
]
. Yet, the Wick rotation trick, normally used to pass from one signature to another does not work for non-local theories
[
21
]
at least in a straightforward way.
.
Mathematical approaches to perturbative quantum field theory
[
10
]
, based on the Einstein causality can not be applied directly to non-local theories.

Therefore, less standard ideas turn out to be useful for non-local theories.
In this paper we deal with the so-called Hamiltonian approach, first suggested in
[
3
]
and further developed in
[
8
,
11
]
. The basic idea is to apply the standard Hamiltonian perturbation theory to a non-local interaction part of the Hamiltonian. As usual, it produces a time-ordered product of interacting Hamiltonians, integrated over spacetime (see Section
4
).
Unlike the local theories, here the time-ordered product makes sense without UV regularization. The integration over spacetime is instead divergent and has to be regularized through the multiplication of all coupling constants with the adiabatic cut-off function depending on the spacetime coordinates and vanishing far away from the origin. This is a typical trick of perturbative quantum field theory, unavoidable in general due to the Haag theorem
[
2
]
. To obtain a physically meaningful result one has to pass to the limit of the adiabatic cut-off function going to the unity, known as the adiabatic limit.

Among the advantages of the Hamiltonian approach are the explicit expression for the scattering operator and its manifest unitarity
2
2

2

See, however, Remark
4.22
.

. A notable restriction of this method is that all theories constructed in this way (if they are not local) have a selected reference frame, in which residual locality persists (see Remark
4.22
).

Originally in
[
11
,
8
]
the Hamiltonian approach was applied to a class of non-local quantum field theories built as an effective description of quantum field theories in the Doplicher-Frednhagen-Roberts quantum (non-commutative) spacetime defined in
[
3
]
. The latter is a simple Lorentz-covariant realization of the physically motivated spacetime uncertainty relations also derived in
[
3
]
. A set of Feynman rules for this theory was derived in
[
8
]
and it was shown that the non-locality smears out the typical ultraviolet singularities of local theories. However, it was observed that the adiabatic limit of the scattering operator (so-called strong adiabatic limit) does not exist in general due to the external lines correction. This is not a surprise. In fact, in the local case, the strong adiabatic limit exists only when particular renormalization conditions are specified
[
10
]
.

In the author’s Ph.D. thesis
[
12
]
a class of non-local quantum ultraviolet finite Hamiltonian perturbative Quantum Field Theories (HpQFT) was defined. This class includes the theories on the Doplicher-Fredenhagen-Roberts quantum spacetime considered in
[
8
,
11
]
. It was shown that the weak adiabatic limit always exists as in the local case.

In this paper, we reproduce this result using significantly different techniques for the computations. Namely, instead of unbounded operators (operator-valued functions, operator-valued distributions, etc.) on the Hilbert space, we deal with continuous operators on the spaces of Schwartz functions. Then the vectors, vector-valued, operator-valued functions, distributions, and parameter-dependent distributions can be treated uniformly as linear continuous operators between the spaces of Schwartz functions. Furthermore, we introduce the second quantization map (see Subsection
3.4
), allowing us to do all computations with the spaces of a fixed finite number of particles only. Naïve formulas, such as the Wick theorem and its generalizations for operator-valued distributions can be provided with a direct sense in this formalism. Here we use only a small part of this framework. In the subsequent paper devoted to the strong adiabatic limit, a suitable generalization of this formalism will be used much more intensively. From this perspective, besides giving an improved exposition of the constructions of
[
12
]
this paper may be considered as an illustration of this new formalism.

The paper is organized as follows. The rest of this section contains a summary of the notation and terminology used. In the second Section we explain how different objects, such as vector-valued, operator-valued, and parameter-dependent (as well as mixes of these kinds) distributions can be treated uniformly as linear operators between appropriately chosen spaces. In the third Section, the standard domain of the quantum field theory
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
is defined. We also construct classes of operators, operator-valued functions, distributions, etc. on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
and introduce the second quantization procedure. In the Fourth Section, motivated by the time-dependent perturbation field theory in quantum mechanics, we describe the class of non-local quantum field theories we deal with. In the last, fifth Section, the weak adiabatic limit existence is formulated and proved. The Appendix contains several formulations of Feynman rules in the adiabatic limit convenient for practical computations.

Acknowledgements

This work is a reformulation and continuation of the PhD project and later partially supported by the PostDoc fellowship, both granted by the University of Rome “Tor Vergata” and performed under the advisory of G. Morsella.

1.1
Conventions, notation and terminology

General

Three-dimensional vectors semantically related to the physical coordinate or momentum space are denoted with the vector symbol, e.g.
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Other finite-dimensional vectors are denoted with bold letters, e..
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We use the standard notation

\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{x}^{\alpha}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}}

for the partial derivatives, where
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
is a multi-index and
|\alpha|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}
.

The permutation group of
k
elements is denoted with
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. A permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
is understood as a functions
\{1,\ldots,k\}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
i\mapsto\sigma(i)=\sigma_{i}
.

We use the following abbreviated notations for lists of repeating variables:

x_{1\ldots n}\Longleftrightarrow x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}

x_{n\ldots k}\Longleftrightarrow x_{n},x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{k},

x_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{k}}\Longleftrightarrow x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(k)},\,(\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}),

(x_{i})_{i=1\ldots n}\Longleftrightarrow x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},

(x_{i,j})_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots m_{j}}\Longleftrightarrow x_{1,1},x_{1,2},\ldots,x_{1,m_{1}},x_{2,1},x_{2,2},\ldots,x_{2,m_{2}},\ldots,x_{n,m_{n}}.

The last two notations are used for more composite construction depending on
i
(or
(i,j)
) in place of
x_{i}
(or
x_{i,j}
). Inside integrals we use

d^{nk}\bm{x}_{1\ldots k}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}d^{n}\bm{x}_{j}

for
\bm{x}_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that we always indicate the dimensions of the space explicitly. We write
\bm{x}_{1\ldots k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to express that
\bm{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
in contrast with
(\bm{x}_{1\ldots k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}
.

For two topological spaces
X
,
Y
we use
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
to denote the space of all linear continuous operators from
X
to
Y
. We set
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mathcal{L}(X,X)
.

If
f
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
we may interchangeably write
f(\bm{x},\bm{y})
or
f(\bm{z})
for
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
.

We use the ordering convention for non-commutative products

\prod_{i=n}^{m}A_{i}=A_{n}A_{n+1}\cdots A_{n+m}.

Quantum field theories

By a quantum field theory we in general mean a (local or non-local, not necessarily Lorentz-covariant) bosonic real scalar perturbative quantum field theory. All results can be easily generalized to the case of several particle species and the Fermionic statistics. The spin plays no role in absence of the Lorentz invariance, so different polarizations can be treated as separate species. The same can be said about the antiparticles in absence of the locality.

By a wave function we mean a vector of state of such theory with a fixed number of particles and presented as a function of the momenta.

The Quantum Field theories we consider are not necessarily Lorentz-invariant, both because no reasonable non-local Lorentz-invariant interaction is known and due to immanent breaking of Lorentz invariance by the non-local Hamiltonian formalism (see Remark
4.22
). Yet, in
[
8
,
11
]
the free theory is assumed to be Lorentz-invariant. We instead allow it to be broken from the beginning as it produces no additional technical problems. For this reason, we need to deal with generalized dispersion relations. We require that both the energy and its inverse are smooth functions of the momentum which have at most polynomial growth at infinity together with all their partial derivatives. The space of such functions
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
is defined in Subsection
2.6
.

Definition 1.2
.

By a
(massive) dispersion function
we mean a positive function
\omega_{0}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
such that
(\vec{k}\mapsto\frac{1}{\omega_{0}(\vec{k})})\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, and
3
3

3

The
M>0
condition is introduced to eliminate exotic dispersion functions of polynomial decaying at the infinity. The possibility of zero energy at a finite point is already excluded by the smoothness of the inverse energy.

M=\inf_{\vec{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\omega_{0}({\vec{k}})>0.

The constant
M
is called the
mass
of the dispersion function. We always assume
\omega_{0}(-\vec{p})=\omega_{0}(\vec{p})
for the sake of simplicity. This condition can be easily relaxed.

Feynman graphs and Feynman rules

We use the Feynman graphs to keep track of combinatorics in operator products computations.

The terminology used is mostly standard and intuitive, but it makes sense to summarize it in one place. We introduce various versions of the Feynman rules both in the main text and in Appendix based on Feynman graphs with different labeling and auxiliary structures.

In general, a
Feynman graph
is a graph with marked vertices and edges (interchangeably called lines). We always exclude the graphs containing an edge starting and ending at the same vertex.

We consider
partially
or
totally ordered
and
unordered
Feynman graphs, which come with partial, total or no order on the set of vertices respectively. When dealing with ordered graphs, we use terms like
precede
,
earlier
,
earliest
and so on (respectively,
follows
,
later
,
latest
etc.) to describe the relative order of vertices and use the symbols
\prec
and
\succ
respectively for these two relations
4
4

4

This terminology have clear interpretation if we keep in mind that each vertex corresponds to an operator. Then earlier vertices correspond to operators which act first. In computations of the Green functions (and the scattering amplitudes
[
12
]
) the order can be physically interpreted as the order of the “elementary scatterings” in the physical time. The physical interpretation is lost in the Wightman functions computation, where some parts of the operator product are anti-time ordered.
.

A total order, when prescribed, induces the natural (up to an overall flip) orientation of the edges, making the graph directed acyclic. For definiteness, we choose the orientation from the earlier to the later vertices. Partial orders in general do not define orientations of edges, but we consider only such of them that do, and moreover are generated by an acyclic orientation of the edges
5
5

5

In other words, instead of partially ordered graphs we should be speaking of directed acyclic ones. But the partial order plays a major role in the Feynman rules, which we have emphasized in the terminology.
. In the figures we always draw earlier vertices to the left of the later ones.

We distinguish external and internal vertices. The external vertices may be incident to only one line, for internal ones there are no limitations
6
6

6

Of course, the contribution of a diagram may vanish if the corresponding term is absent in the interaction.
.

The vertices can be enumerated or not, and the numeration can agree with the order or not. When it does, rather counter-intuitively, in ordered graphs we enumerate the vertices from the
last to the earliest
7
7

7

This choice of numeration is dictated by the traditional right-to-left composition notation and numeration of the factors in products from left to right
.

To the lines we often assign a flux of the momentum and sometimes the energy flow. The flow is directed, i.e. it is incoming for some vertices and outgoing for others. Graphically we draw an arrow parallel to the line to show the flow direction. For ordered graphs we assume that the flux always goes from the earlier to the later vertex. We call the difference of the sum of all outgoing and the sum of all incoming momenta (respectively, energy) at a vertex the
momentum
(respectively,
energy
)
defect
of the vertex. In some cases we make a distinguishing between the on-shell (i.e. constrained by a dispersion relation) and off-shell (i.e. an independent free variable) energies, and respectively the on-shell and off-shell energy defects.

We say that a connected component of a Feynman graph is a
vacuum component
if it contains no external vertices.

The
Feynman rules
consist of the following ingredients:

•

Description of the classes of relevant Feynman graphs (e.g. ordered graphs with a fixed number of internal and external enumerated vertices with no vacuum energy corrections);

•

Labeling rules, i.e. the list of free variables assigned to each line (e.g. spatial momentum) or each vertex (e.g. timestamp);

•

Prescription o the factors corresponding to each line and each vertex;

•

The integration and summation range for the free parameters and additional overall factor if necessary.

To each graph corresponds an expression constructed as a product of factors corresponding to the components of the graph according to the Feynman rules and the overall factor (if it is present), integrated and summed with respect to the free parameters and
divided by the order of the automorphisms group of the graph
8
8

8

We assume that the automorphisms preserve all defined structure of the graphs (including order and numeration of vertices if they are defined) except for free parameters assignment. The combinatoric factors are not at all important for the weak adiabatic limit, so we do not discuss the symmetry factor, but it can always be reconstructed. Yet, we note that this factor is different from the one of
[
8
]
because of different normalization of the vertex factors. Our conventions instead agree with the standard physics textbooks, e.g.
[
13
]
to facilitate eventual comparisons.
.

We say that some object can be computed by Feynman rules if it is equal to the sum of expressions corresponding to all relevant graphs according to that Feynman rules.

Formal power series

Let
g
be a formal parameter.
For a vector space
X
, the space of
X
-valued formal power series in terms of
g
is defined as the vector space of
X
-valued functions on
\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

X\llbracket g\rrbracket=X^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}=\{x:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow X\}

with pointwise linear operations. Elements of
X\llbracket g\rrbracket
we symbolically present as

x(g)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{(n)}g^{n}.

If
X
,
Y
and
Z
are vector spaces and
*:X\times Y\rightarrow Z
is a bilinear operation, then for
x\in X\llbracket g\rrbracket
,
y\in Y\llbracket g\rrbracket
we set

(x*y)(g)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{n}x_{(m)}*x_{(n-m)}\right)g^{n}\in Z\llbracket g\rrbracket.

The limits involving formal power series should be understood in the sense of pointwise convergence of the underlying functions on
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Consequently, integrals of power series are defined.

To avoid confusion we write

(x(g))_{g^{n}}
instead of
x_{n}

Test functions, distributions, and related objects

We use the standard symbol for the space of Schwartz function
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and the space of tempered distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathbb{C})
dual to it (here
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right)=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right)=\mathbb{C}
for convenience). The seminorms of
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
are denoted as follows:

||f||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),m,k}=\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},|a|\leq k}(1+||\bm{x}||)^{m}|\partial^{a}f(\bm{x})|,\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

We often omit the adjective tempered, as no other classes of distributions appear in this paper.
We use the square brackets to denote the evaluation of a distribution on a test function (or, more generally, the action of an operator on a vector
9
9

9

In selected cases we omit the square bracket in this sense.
). The round brackets after a symbol of distribution are used in the symbolic integral notation which is explained in Subsection
2.8
.

A lot of non-standard notation is introduced in Section
2
. In particular, the operator
\mathfrak{e}
appears in Subsection
2.1
; the operation
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}{}
is explained Subsection
2.3
; the operations
\mathfrak{I}
and
\mathfrak{R}
is introduced in Subsection
2.4
; the operations
\mathfrak{A}
and the tensor product
\otimes
are defined in Subsection
2.13
. Most of the other operators used in the paper can be found in Subsection
2.7

For the secondary quantization map
A\mapsto\widehat{A}
, and an explanation of the underlining notation see Subsection
3.4
. As a general rule, the hated underlined symbols denote the objects in their standard sense. For example, one can think of
\underline{\widehat{\phi}}_{0}
as the usual free quantum field, defined as an operator-valued distribution. The original object
\phi_{0}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}{})
(notation of Section
3
is used) does not have a direct sense but is convenient to operate with.

2
Framework for operator-valued parameter-dependent distributions

As usual in Quantum Field Theory, operator-valued distributions are essential for construction. Standard general formalism (e.g.
[
14
,
15
]
) is, however, not suitable for our needs, because we deal with a certain class of operators and distributions only. In particular, instead of the Hilbert space, we may always deal with finer spaces of Schwartz functions
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This section is devoted to
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
-valued functions and (in general, parameter-dependent) distributions. In Section
3
we explain how this construction leads to the usual notion of distributions, valued in unbounded operators on the Hilbert space.

The material presented here is not original, but nowhere presented in the form suitable enough for us. In the parts devoted to parameter-dependent distributions and restrictions of distributions we mostly follow
[
15
]
. The later concept is an older “cheap” version of the microlocal analysis
[
16
]
, allowing to restrict the distribution to fixed values of the parameters. There are several reasons why we stick to it. First of all, it is just simpler to verify the existence conditions in the situations we deal with (especially taking into account that otherwise we would be forced to use generalized to the tempered distributions case). The second reason is that this way our framework is more uniform, allowing to treat vector-valued and parameter-dependent distributions together. Finally, the strong adiabatic limit, which is not considered here, but will be presented in the subsequent paper
[
22
]
, requires more general spaces, and hence the microlocal analysis would require further generalization. Instead, we aim to use as few properties of the space
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
as possible
10
10

10

In broad terms, we use only that it is Fr‘echet and that the partial evaluations are continuous in a siutable sense
.

In description of vector-valued functions we partially follow
[
18
]
. The part on the multiplier spaces follows
[
17
]
.

2.1
Vector-valued test functions

We start by the following observation
11
11

11

See chapter 40 of
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
there is an isomorphism between the following topological vector spaces

1.

The space
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
;

2.

The space

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))=

\left\{f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\Big|||f||_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)),s,k,l,r}<\infty,\quad\forall l,r,s,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\},

where for a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
and
l,r,s,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we set

||f||_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)),s,k,l,r}=\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},|a|\leq k}(1+||\bm{x}||)^{s}||(\partial^{\alpha}f)(\bm{x})||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),l,r}

(2.1)

with the topology induced by these seminorms.

The isomorphism is given by
\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right),\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)))
:

\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}f(\bm{x})(\bm{y})=f(\bm{x},\bm{y}),\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

\left(\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}\right)^{-1}g(\bm{x},\bm{y})=g(\bm{x})(\bm{y}),\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Proof.

The linear map
\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}
is clearly well-defined.
We have to check that
(\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m})^{-1}
is indeed valued in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
, or more precisely the joint continuity of
(\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m})^{-1}g
together with its partial derivatives for any
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. For that we write

|g(\bm{x}^{\prime})(\bm{y}^{\prime})-g(\bm{x})(\bm{y})|\leq|g(\bm{x}^{\prime})(\bm{y})-g(\bm{x})(\bm{y})|+|g(\bm{x}^{\prime})(\bm{y}^{\prime})-g(\bm{x}^{\prime})(\bm{y})|\leq

||g||_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)),0,1,0,0}||\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}||+||g||_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)),0,0,0,1}||\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}||,

\forall\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

The right-hand sides can be made anyhow small for sufficiently small
||\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}||
and
||\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}||
, so
g
is jointly smooth. The derivatives can be treated in the same way. The continuity of
\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{n+1\ldots n+m}^{n+m}
follows by the standard inequalities

(1+||(\bm{x},\bm{y})||)\leq(1+||\bm{x}||)(1+||\bm{y}||),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

(2.2)

(1+||\bm{x}||)\leq(1+||(\bm{x},\bm{y})||),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

(2.3)

(1+||\bm{y}||)\leq(1+||(\bm{x},\bm{y})||),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

∎

We conclude that the space
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
can be interpreted as the space of
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
-valued Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The identification is not unique, in general we define
\mathfrak{e}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}}^{n+m}
extracting the arguments on positions
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}
as parameters. This operator is also of great use in the technical definition below in this section.

In what follows we do all real computations with more understandable space
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
, but sometimes use the aforementioned interpretation to streamline the presentation and to simplify comparison with standard techniques.

2.2
Vector-valued and parameter-dependent distributions

There are two related spaces important for Section
3
.
First one may consider the space of
vector-valued distributions

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
which is natural to identify with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
.
The second one is the space of
parameter-dependent distributions of Schwartz class
12
12

12

We use terminology of
[
15
]

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. Following
[
15
]
, we define it as a space of maps
\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

F:\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni\bm{x}\rightarrow F_{y}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

such that

(y\mapsto F_{y}[f])\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

Indeed, it is nothing but a tautological rewriting of differentiability and fast decay (together with all partial derivatives) properties of
F
, considered as a distribution-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
13
13

13

Recall that we assume the weak-* topology for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, so all limits including
F
should be computed after evaluation on a test function.
.

These two notions coincide.

Remark 2.2

(Variant of Exercise 2.43 of
[
15
]
)
.

Fix
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is a natural bijection between
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. It is given by identifying

F_{\bm{x}}[f]=F[f](\bm{x}),\quad,\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),

where in the left and the right-hand sides
F
is treated as a parameter-dependent and vector-valued distribution.

Proof.

The only thing to prove is that a parameter-dependent distribution of class
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
defines a continuous map
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
. As
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
are Fréchet spaces, by the closed graph theorem, it is enough to show for any sequence
f_{j}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
j=1,\ldots,n
the conditions

f_{j}\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}{f}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),

(2.4)

(y\mapsto F_{y}[f_{j}])\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}{g}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),

(2.5)

imply
g=(y\mapsto F_{y}[f])
. Since convergence in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
implies the pointwise one, (
2.4
-
2.5
) and the continuity of
F_{y}
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
give

g(y)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}F_{y}[f_{j}]=F_{y}[f],\,\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

as needed.
∎

From now on we make no distinguishing between vector-valued and parameter-dependent distributions and use the notation
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
for both.

Remark 2.3
.

Combining Remarks
2.2
and
2.1
, for
m,n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we can interpret
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
as the space of parameter-dependent
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
-valued distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of class
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
.

2.3
Operator-valued (parameter-dependent) distributions

Remark 2.4
.

Take
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
There is a correspondence between elements of the space
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
-valued distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It is given by defining for each
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
an operator-valued distribution
\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)))
by

\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}F[f][\psi]=F[f\otimes\psi],\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall\psi\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right).

Here we assume the pointwise topology on
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
.

Proof.

For
f,\psi
as in the statement, the map
\psi\mapsto f\otimes\psi
is continuous, so

\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}F[f]\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)),\quad,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

Similarly, we get continuity of the map
f\mapsto F[f][\psi]
for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
making
\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}F
into a distribution.

Conversely, any distribution
G\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)))
defines a separately continuous bilinear map

\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),

\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\ni(f,\psi)\mapsto G[f][\psi].

Each such map is jointly continuous (corollary of Theorem 34.1 in
[
18
]
) and defines
{\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}}^{-1}G
such that

{\mathfrak{E}_{1,\ldots,n}}^{-1}G[f\otimes\psi]=G[f][\psi].

∎

Remark 2.5
.

Combining this result with Remark
2.1
, for
n,m,k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we may interpret
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k+l}\right))
as the space of
parameter-dependent operator-valued distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, taking values in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
of class
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{l}\right)
.

As in Subsection
2.1
the identification is not unique and, in general, we define
\mathfrak{E}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}F
analogously.

As before, we prefer to perform computations within the spaces of linear operators, but use the interpretation presented above to make their sense more clear.

2.4
Restriction of a distribution to fixed values of arguments

Occasionally we prefer to treat some of the arguments interchangeably as distributional one parametric ones. It is easy to make the latter into the former using the following operation.

Notation 2.6
.

Take
n,k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,m+k\}
,
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n+k\}
and

F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k+m}\right))
. We define

\mathfrak{I}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k+n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)),\quad\mathfrak{I}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}F[f](\bm{y})=

(2.6)

\int\mathfrak{e}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{m+k}\left[F\left[\left(\mathfrak{e}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{n+k}\left[f\right]\right)\left(\bm{x}\right)(\bm{y})\right]\right](\bm{x})(\bm{y})d^{k}\bm{x},\quad\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

The meaning of this operator is that it makes the parameters on the places
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
into distributional arguments inserted on positions
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k}
.
To understand the meaning of the rather formal construction above let us consider a simple example.

Example 2.7
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
. Then

\mathfrak{I}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}h[f]=\int{h(\bm{x})f(\bm{x})d^{m}\bm{x}}.

We introduce the partially defined inverse operation

\mathfrak{R}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{i_{1\ldots k}}=\left(\mathfrak{I}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}\right)^{-1}.

If for
G\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
the operator
\mathfrak{R}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{i_{1\ldots k}}G

exists, we call it a
restriction of
G
because effectively it allows us to evaluate
G
at fixed values of its distributional arguments.

Remark 2.8
.

Here we again follow
[
15
]
. Note that the microlocal analysis suggests a more sophisticated notion of restriction. One may show that whenever both are applicable, the results coincide, but the existence conditions are not in general equivalent.

Proposition 2.9

(Analog of Proposition 2.11 in
[
15
]
)
.

For the existence of

\mathfrak{R}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{i_{1\ldots k}}G

it is sufficient to find a map
F:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
(not a priori continuous) such that
G[f]=\mathfrak{I}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}F[f]
where the right-hand sides is formally defined by (
2.6
).

Proof.

The reasoning is similar to Remark
2.2
.
Without any loss of generality, we assume

i_{j}=i^{\prime}_{j}=j,\quad j=1,\ldots,k.

Assume
G\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
and
F
is as in the statement. Our goal is to prove that
F
is continuous.
To do that we evaluate
G
on a tensor product

G[h\otimes g](\bm{y})=\mathfrak{I}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F[h\otimes g](\bm{y})=\int{F[h](\bm{x},\bm{y})g(\bm{x})d^{k}\bm{x}},

(2.7)

\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\forall h\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

and apply the closed graph theorem. Indeed, assume that there is a sequence
h_{j}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}h_{j}=h,\,\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}F[h_{j}]=u.

(2.8)

On the one hand, for any
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
by (
2.7
,
2.8
) we get

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}G[h_{j}\otimes g]=G[h\otimes g].

(2.9)

On the other hand, the continuity of
G
and
h\mapsto h\otimes g
for any
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
together with (
2.8
) gives

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}G[h_{j}\otimes g](\bm{y})=\int{u(\bm{x},\bm{y})g(\bm{x})d^{k}\bm{x}}.

(2.10)

Combining (
2.9
) with (
2.10
), we conclude that whenever (
2.8
) is satisfied,

\int{u(\bm{x},\bm{y})g(\bm{x})d^{k}\bm{x}}=\int{F[h](\bm{x},\bm{y})g(x)d^{k}\bm{x}},\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

By assumption
F[h],u\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
, so the last identity is possible only if
F[h]=u
as desired.
∎

2.5
Augmentations and products

Developing Remark
2.1
we may act by an operator from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
on a vector from
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right)
treating the rest
k
arguments as parameters. This leads to the following construction.

Remark 2.10
.
14
14

14

Variant of Theorem 2.1.3 in
[
16
]
.

Let
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
.
Then we may introduce

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
as

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F[f](\bm{x},\bm{y})=F[\mathfrak{e}_{1,\ldots,k}^{n+k}f(\bm{x})](\bm{y}),\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\,\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\,\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

(2.11)

More generally, take two sequences of pairwise different integers

i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n+k\},\qquad i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,m+k\}.

Then there is
\mathfrak{A}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
defined by

\mathfrak{A}_{i_{1\ldots k}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}F=\left(\mathfrak{e}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{m+k}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\ni\bm{x}\mapsto F[\mathfrak{e}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{n+k}f(\bm{x})]\right).

(2.12)

Proof.

Clearly, it is enough to consider
\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F
. Equivalence of (
2.12
) and (
2.11
) follows by construction of
\left(\mathfrak{e}_{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}}^{m+k}\right)^{-1}
in Remark
2.1
.

By Remark
2.2
we only have to show that the right-hand sides of (
2.11
) is in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
as a function of
(\bm{x},\bm{y})
and is continuous with respect to
f
when
(\bm{x},\bm{y})
is fixed. Both facts follow from Remark
2.1
and continuity of
F
(which in particular allows to commute it with derivatives with respect to
\bm{x}
and estimate seminorms of
F[\mathfrak{e}_{1,\ldots,k}^{n+k}f(\bm{x})]
in terms of the ones of
f
).
∎

Remark 2.11
.

We use the same notation as in the previous remark.
Equivalently
\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F
can be characterised as a unique operator in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
and
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
holds

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F[f\otimes g]=g\otimes F[g].

(2.13)

Proof.

Clearly, (
2.11
) implies (
2.13
). Uniqueness of the operator satisfying (
2.13
) follows by density of
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right)
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

From the last remark follows that “operators acting on different variables commute”. In particular, for
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
and
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)\right)
we have
F\otimes F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+m^{\prime}}\right)\right)
defined as

F\otimes F^{\prime}=\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n+1,\ldots,n+m^{\prime}}^{n+1,\ldots,n+m^{\prime}}F\right)\circ\left(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,n}^{1,\ldots,n}F^{\prime}\right)=\left(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}F^{\prime}\right)\circ\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n+1,\ldots,n+n^{\prime}}^{n+1,\ldots,n+n^{\prime}}F\right).

This operation coincides with the ordinary tensor product of test functions for
n=n^{\prime}=0
and of distributions for
m=m^{\prime}=0
.

Remark 2.13
.

The augmentations can be expressed via the tensor product introduced above. In particular, let
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then

\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n+1,\ldots,n+k}^{m+1,\ldots,m+k}F\right)=F\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))},

\left(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F\right)=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))}\otimes F.

Remark 2.14
.

The following two simple properties of augmentation are useful:

•

Consecutive augmentations can be combined, e.g. for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,l}^{1,\ldots,l}(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F)=\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,l+k}^{1,\ldots,l+k}F,\,\forall k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0};

•

Augmentation is distributive with respect to compositions, e.g. for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
,
G\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}(F\circ G)=(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F)\circ(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}G),\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

•

As a consequence of the above and Remark
2.12
, if
n,n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime},m,m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

F\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right),\,F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right)\right),

G\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right),\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)\right),

then

(F\otimes G)\circ(F^{\prime}\otimes G^{\prime})=(F\circ F^{\prime})\otimes(G\circ G^{\prime})
.

2.6
Multipliers

For now, we considered only parameter-dependent distributions fast decaying at the high values of the parameters. This condition is too restrictive. For example, the free quantum field is known to be an operator-valued distribution restrictable to the fixed values of time (see
[
14
]
for standard treatment and Subsection
3.4
for construction in our formalism) but it does not decay in time. In this subsection we define a large enough space containing such objects.

We recall that the space of multipliers
[
15
]

\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
consists of functions having limited growth together with all their derivatives,

\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})|\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,\exists m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},|a|\leq k}(1+||x||)^{-m}|\partial^{a}f(x)|\leq\infty\}.

The name comes from the following alternative characterization of
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. For a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
define pointwise multiplication by
f
as

(\mathfrak{M}^{f}g)(\bm{x})=f(\bm{x})g(\bm{x})\,(\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Then
15
15

15

See
[
15
]
and
[
17
]
, or generalization of this fact in Remark
2.15
below.

\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)=\{f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}|\mathfrak{M}^{f}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))\}.

Note that by Remark
2.2
it is enough to show that
\mathfrak{M}^{f}
leaves
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
invariant. We also define a complementary operator
\mathfrak{M}_{g}:\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
defined for each
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and defined by

\mathfrak{M}_{g}[f]=f\cdot g.

There is a natural topology on
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
generated by seminorms
16
16

16

We follow
[
17
]
.

||f||_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),g,m,k}=||\mathfrak{M}_{g}f||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),m,k},\quad g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

making all
\mathfrak{M}_{g}
for each
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
continuous.
Conversely, for some topological vector space
X
an operator
F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
is continuous whenever

\mathfrak{M}_{g}\circ F\in\mathcal{L}(X,\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)),\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

This way we can always replace
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
-valued operators with
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
ones. We postpone it to Remark
2.17
to introduce first a generalization of the space of multipliers.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we introduce the mixed space
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
as

\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+m})|\forall k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,

\exists r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},y\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
|a|+|b|\leq k\end{subarray}}(1+||\bm{x}||)^{l}(1+||\bm{y}||)^{-r}|\partial_{\bm{x}}^{a}\partial_{\bm{y}}^{b}f(\bm{x},\bm{y})|\leq\infty\}.

Remark 2.15
.

There is an equivalent
characterization of
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}{0}
as a space of all functions
f
that define a map
\mathfrak{M}^{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
by pointwise multiplication

\mathfrak{M}^{f}[g](\bm{x},\bm{y})=f(\bm{x},\bm{y})g(\bm{y}).

Furthermore,

\mathfrak{M}^{f}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)),\forall f\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right).

For any
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
there is a map
\mathfrak{M}_{g}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\right))
such that

\mathfrak{M}_{g}^{(n)}[f]=\mathfrak{M}^{f}[g],

where the topology on
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
is defined by

||f||_{\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),g,l,k}=||\mathfrak{M}^{f}[g]||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right),l,k},\quad g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\,k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Conversely, for an operator
F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
with some topological vector space
X
we have
F\in\mathcal{L}(X,\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
if and only if

\mathfrak{M}^{g}\circ F\in\mathcal{L}(X,\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right))\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right).

Proof.
17
17

17

The presented proof follows
[
17
]
with minimal changes.

If
f\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
and
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
, then
\mathfrak{M}^{f}[g]\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
essentially due to (
2.2
). Conversely, if a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
induces a map
\mathfrak{M}^{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
, then by Remark
2.2
this map is continuous. Let
g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
be such that

g(\bm{x})=1,\forall\bm{x}\in U

for some neighborhood of the origin
U
and for
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
set
g_{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
,
g_{\bm{y}}(\bm{x})=g(\bm{x}-\bm{y})
. Then

f(\bm{x})=\mathfrak{M}^{f}[g_{y}](\bm{x}),\quad\forall\bm{x}\in\bm{y}+U.

The smoothness of
f
is clear. By continuity of
\mathfrak{M}^{f}
for any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n+m}
and any
r\in\mathbb{N}{0}
there are some
C>0
and
l,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

|\partial_{\alpha}f(\bm{x})|(1+||\bm{x}||)^{r}\leq C||g_{\bm{y}}||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),l,b}.

Finally, for some
C^{\prime}>0

||g_{\bm{y}}||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),l,b}\leq C^{\prime}(1+||\bm{y}||)^{l}

by

1+||\bm{x}-\bm{y}||\leq(1+||\bm{x}||)(1+||\bm{y}||).

Thus
f\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
. The rest of the statement is trivial.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

One can prove an analogue of Remark
2.1
, identifying the
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
-valued vector functions of class
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
with elements of
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
.

Remark 2.17
.

The key strategy of dealing with the operators like

F:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)

for
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is to always work with
\mathfrak{M}_{f}\circ F
instead, where
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. For example, similarly to Subsection
2.2
we may consider the space of parameter-dependent distributions
\mathcal{O_{M}}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
defined along the same lines as
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. Any
F\in\mathcal{O_{M}}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
defines a map
F:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
which by Remark
2.15

is continuous if
\mathfrak{M}_{f}\circ F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
for every
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, for which we use Remark
2.2
. This way we get that Subsections
2.2
-
2.4
can be generalized to the case then the target space is of the form
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
,
m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

We should separately take care of the augmentations of Subsection
2.5
, because in general operators acting from the multiplier and mixed spaces may appear. We consider only one simple case, the generalizaiton is straightforward.

Example 2.18
.

Let
m,n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. Then
\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F
can be continued to an element of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
such that

\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F[g](\bm{x},\bm{y})=F[\mathfrak{e}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{m+k}g(\bm{y})](\bm{x}),

(2.14)

\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\forall g\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right).

Alternatively, it can be defined by the relation

\mathfrak{M}_{f}^{(n)}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F=\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F\circ\mathfrak{M}_{f}^{(m)},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),

(2.15)

where in the right-hand sides
\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right))
is defined as in Subseciton
2.5
.

Proof.

First take some
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
evaluate the right-hand sides of (
2.15
) on a function
g\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
:

\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F\circ\mathfrak{M}_{f}^{(m)}[g](\bm{x},\bm{y})=F[\mathfrak{e}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{m+k}g(\bm{y})](\bm{x})f(\bm{y}).

The left-hand side is a Schwartz function of
(\bm{x},\bm{y})
, so pointwise multiplication by a function
\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F[g]
defined by (
2.14
) induces a map
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k+n}\right)
, so by Remark
2.15
we have
\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F[g]\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
and it automatically is the unique solution of (
2.15
). The right-hand side of (
2.15
) belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
, so applying once again Remark
2.15
we get
\mathfrak{A}_{m+1,\ldots,m+k}^{n+1,\ldots,n+k}F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
.
∎

2.7
Elementary examples

A lot of standard operations often arising in the distributions theory and QFT are operators in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}},\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)\right)
for some
n,m,n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We have already constructed the pointwise multiplication operators. Here we list some other classes of operators which are convenient to use later in the text.

Partial derivatives

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
we have
\partial^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
. Likewise, the derivatives can be defined for

\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

Integration with moving boundary

We can define
J_{+},J_{-}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right))
and
J\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right))
as

J_{-}[f](t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t)dt,\,\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right);

(2.16)

J_{+}[f](t)=\int_{t}^{+\infty}f(t)dt,\,\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right);

(2.17)

J[f](t,t^{\prime})=\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}f(t)dt,\,\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).

(2.18)

These operators interact with the derivatives in obvious ways, e.g.

\partial\circ J_{+}=-J_{+}\circ\partial=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right))},

\partial_{1}\circ J=-\partial_{2}\circ J=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right))}.

(2.19)

It is worth noting that the trivial property
J_{+}[f](t)=\lim_{t^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty}J[f](t,t^{\prime})
survives the augmentation, i.e. for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
we have

\lim_{t^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{e}_{2}^{1}(\mathfrak{A}_{2,\ldots,n}^{3,\ldots,n+1}J[f])(t^{\prime})=\mathfrak{A}_{2,\ldots,n}^{2,\ldots,n}J_{+}[f],

(2.20)

and similarly for analogous relations.

For future use we introduce more complicated operators

J^{(n)}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right))

and
J^{(n)_{\pm}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))

defined by

J^{(n)}[f](t,t^{\prime})=\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}dt_{1}\int_{t}^{t_{2}}dt_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n}f(t_{1\ldots n}),\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R};

J^{(n)_{+}}[f](t)=\int_{t}^{+\infty}dt_{1}\int_{t}^{t_{2}}dt_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n}f(t_{1\ldots n}),\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}

J^{(n)_{-}}[f](t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{t}^{t_{2}}dt_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n}f(t_{1\ldots n}),\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

Analogues of (
2.20
) can be written for this class of operators.

Remark 2.19
.

If we treat
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right)
as
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
and think of
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
as a subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the partial derivatives with respect to the parameters define partial derivatives of the vector-valued functions with respect to the parameters. Moreover, the integration with respect to the parameters
18
18

18

Here we suppose that the first
k
arguments are parameters as in Subsection
2.13
such as

\mathfrak{A}_{k+1,\ldots,k+n}^{k+1,\ldots,k+n}J[f]\,(f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right))

coincides with the Bochner integral of the vector-valued function
\mathfrak{e}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}f
. This follows from approximation of
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right)
by

f=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g_{n}\otimes h_{n},g_{n}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),h_{n}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

and then an approximation of
h_{n}
by sequences of the simple functions.

Precompositions

Clearly, if
A
is an injective
19
19

19

If
A
is not injective, then
A^{*}
is valued in the multiplier or mixed space
linear operator

20

We limit ourselves to the linear functions for simplicity. In general a function should belong to the multipliers space (see Section

) together with its inverse, see Exercise 2.8 of

]
.

\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then it defines an operator
21
21

21

This notation, of course, is in conflict with the one for adjoint operators. The later, however, is used here only episodically, since another concept of conjugated operator is more important for us. Anyway, we never introduce adjoint of a finite-dimensional operators, so no confusion is possible.

A^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)),

A^{*}[f](\bm{x})=f(A\bm{x}),\,\forall\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

In particular, the diagonal map
D_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

D_{n}(\bm{x})=(\bm{x},\bm{x}),\,\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

defines
{D_{n}}^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
such that
{D_{n}}^{*}f(\bm{x})=f(\bm{x},\bm{x})
for
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Another important class of maps on comes from the permutation group. For
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we set
(\sigma)_{(k)}:\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}
as
(\sigma)_{(k)}(\bm{x}_{1\ldots n})=(\bm{x}_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}),\,\bm{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},j=1,\ldots,n
. We define
\mathfrak{p}^{(k)}_{\sigma}=(\sigma^{-1})_{(k)}^{*}
defining a representation of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}\right)
and

\bm{\Sigma}^{(k)}_{n}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\mathfrak{p}^{(k)}_{\sigma}.

Functions as operators, tensor and pointwise multiplication

Recall that we identify
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
by treating

f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
as a map

\mathbb{C}\ni c\mapsto cf.

The augmentation define the tensor multiplication by
f
, e.g.

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}f[g]=g\otimes f,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

.
The following example illustrates usage of this operation together with the pointwise multiplication of Subsection
2.6
. The subsequent remark points out the issue of symbolic notation of the next subsection which one should be aware of.

Example 2.20
.

There is an important relation between the tensor multiplication above and the pointwise multiplication of Subsection
2.6
. For example, let
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right)
and consider a map
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+k}\right))

F[g](\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})=g(\bm{x},\bm{y})f(\bm{y},\bm{z}),\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\forall\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

On the one hand, we may first tensor with
f

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m+k}^{1,\ldots,m+k}f[g](\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{y^{\prime}},\bm{z})=f(\bm{x},\bm{y})g(\bm{y}^{\prime},\bm{z})\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\forall\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\forall\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and then collapse
\bm{y}
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}
into one variable,

F=\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m,m+2k+1,\ldots,m+2k+n}^{1,\ldots,m,m+k+1,\ldots,m+k+n}{D_{k}}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m+k}^{1,\ldots,m+k}f.

(2.21)

On the other hand, we may start from tensoring with a constant function by
\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m+k}^{1,\ldots,m+k}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+k}\right)),

\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m+k}^{1,\ldots,m+k}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)}[g](\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})=g(\bm{x},\bm{y})\,\forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\forall\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\forall\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and then multiply by
f
, ignoring first
n
variables (
\bm{x}
in the notation above), getting

F=\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,n}^{1,\ldots,n}\mathfrak{M}^{f}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m+k}^{1,\ldots,m+k}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)}.

(2.22)

Remark 2.21
.

In the setting of the previous example the forms (
2.21
) and (
2.22
). However, only the later generalizes easily to the case
f\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Indeed, the map
{D_{k}}^{*}
does maps
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
into
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
rather than
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)
, so in the form (
2.21
) it takes a separate effort to prove that
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+k}\right))
. At the same time,
\mathfrak{M}^{f}
can be replaced by
\mathfrak{M}_{f}
which extends to a continuous operator
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
for obvious reasons.

The Fourier transform

\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}_{n}[f](\bm{x})=\int{e^{\eta\mathrm{i}\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}f(\bm{k})d^{k}\bm{x}},\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is a well-known invertible operator in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Here
\eta=\pm 1
.
Note that
\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}_{n}
has a restriction
\mathfrak{R}_{1,\ldots,n}^{n+1,\ldots,2n}\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n}\right)
,

\mathfrak{R}_{1,\ldots,n}^{n+1,\ldots,2n}\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}_{n}(\bm{x},\bm{k})=e^{i\eta\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}.

(2.23)

2.8
Symbolic notation and calculus

In this subsection we generalize the standard symbolic integral notation from the distributions theory

F[f]=\int F(\bm{x})f(\bm{x})d^{n}\bm{x},\,F\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\,f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

(2.24)

We introduce all the notions and operations for the spaces
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, but use their generalization to the multiplier and mixed spaces as explained in Subsection
2.6
. Before giving it in the full generality let us first make one step from (
2.24
)

Notation 2.22
.

We write

F[f](\bm{y})=\int F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)f(\bm{x})d^{n}\bm{x},

(2.25)

where
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
,
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
We call
\bm{x}
the
distributional argument
in contrast to the
parameters

\bm{y}
of
F
. The compose symbol
F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)
we call the
symbolic kernel
of
F
.

Note that (
2.24
) reduces to (
2.25
) if
m=0
.

Remark 2.23
.

By the Schwartz kernel theorem we can could treat the symbolic kernel of
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
as an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right)
, keeping (
2.25
). But this is not in the line with the goals we set in the beginning of the section: to use minimally special properties of the Schwartz spaces and to highlight that all the objects we deal with are, in one way or another, linear operators between the Schwartz spaces.

The notation (
2.25
) resembles action of a matrix on a vector.
So, it is natural to use the matrix product-like notation for the symbols of compositions of operators.

Notation 2.24
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
j=1,\ldots,n
take
F_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j-1}}\right))
, where
m_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
j=0,\ldots,n
. Then we write

(F_{1}\circ F_{2}\circ\cdots\circ F_{n})\left(\bm{x_{0}}|\bm{x}_{n}\right)=

(2.26)

\int F_{1}\left(\bm{x_{0}}|\bm{x}_{1}\right)F_{1}\left(\bm{x_{1}}|\bm{x}_{2}\right)\cdots F_{n}\left(\bm{x_{n}}|\bm{x}_{n-1}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}d^{m_{j}}\bm{x}_{j},\,\bm{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{0}},\,\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{n}}.

We wrote for one time the product of the symbolic kernels explicitly to underline that it is ordered in a particular way. As these expressions are formal, we have no right to permute the multipliers for now, eventhough essentially they are numerical distributions. At the same time, we may safely use the “Fubini’s theorem” for symbolic integrals by construction (and associativity of the composition). As we make no distinguishment between
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
, we naturally identify
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
with the function
F\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Then Notation
2.24
reduces to Notation
2.22
when
n=2
and
m_{2}=0
. We underline that the ordering of symbolic kernels in (
2.26
) is not arbitrary, instead, it reproduces the order of operators in the composition.

The next step is to introduce separate notation for special classes of operators.

Notation 2.25
.

Fix
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
. We set:

•

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}
,

\partial_{\bm{y}}^{\alpha}F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=(\partial^{\alpha}\circ F)\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)(\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m});

•

For an injective map
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{m})
we set
22
22

22

As before, the non-injective maps are allowed, but lead to multiplier (or mixed) space-valued operators

F\left(\bm{y}|A\bm{x}\right)=({A}^{*}\circ F)\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)\,(\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m});

•

For
f\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
(

) we set
23
23

23

Clearly,
\mathfrak{M}_{f}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)}\circ F\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+k}\right))
. We add this case despite our decision to not discuss the multiplier and mixed spaces, because it concerns the issues not appearing for the Schwartz spaces. We will need this operation to deal with quantum fields and Hamiltonian densities.

f(\bm{y},\bm{z})F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=(\mathfrak{M}_{f}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)}\circ F)\left(\bm{y},\bm{z}|\bm{x}\right)(\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k});

For
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
