(F\otimes F^{\prime})\left(\bm{y},\bm{y^{\prime}}|\bm{x},\bm{x^{\prime}}\right)=F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)F^{\prime}\left(\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right),\,\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}},\bm{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}.

Remark 2.26
.

Let us look on the consistency issues of the notation above.

•

Notation
2.25
becomes tautological if we put
m=0
;

•

If
F
and
A
are as in Notation
2.25
and
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{l},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then

F\left(BA\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)

has two interpretations

((BA)*\circ F)\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)\,\rm{and}\,(B^{*}\circ A^{*}\circ F)\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right).

But clearly
(BA)^{*}=B^{*}\circ A^{*}
, so the interpetations coincide;

•

for
F,F^{\prime},A
as in Notation
2.25
and
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)
the expression

F\left(A\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)F^{\prime}\left(A^{\prime}\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)

has two possible interpretations:

(A\oplus A^{\prime})^{*}\circ(F\otimes F^{\prime})\quad\rm{and}\quad(A^{*}\circ F)\otimes({A^{\prime}}^{*}\circ F^{\prime})=(A^{*}\otimes{A^{\prime}}^{*})\circ(F\otimes F^{\prime}),

where we have used Remark
2.14
to rewrite the second version. By a direct computation
(A\oplus A^{\prime})^{*}=A^{*}\otimes{A^{\prime}}^{*}
, so the interpretations are equivaent;

•

For
F
as in Notation
2.25
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
expression

f(\bm{y},\bm{z})F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)

has two possible interpretations:

(\mathfrak{M}_{f}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,m}^{1,\ldots,m}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)}\circ F)\left(\bm{y},\bm{z}|\bm{x}\right)\,\rm{and}\,\mathfrak{A}_{k+1,\ldots,k+m,2k+m+1,\ldots,2k+m+n}^{k+1,\ldots,k+m+n}{D_{k}}^{*}\circ(f\otimes F).

Here in the first case we treat
f
as an element of
\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)\supset\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right)
, while in the second case as an operator in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{0}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
.
They coincide by Example
2.20
. As explained in Remark
2.21
, the separate definition of pointwise multiplication for the multiplier spaces can not be discarded.

Remark 2.27
.

The operations introduced above satisfy the following:

•

The partial derivatives commute among themselves;

•

Derivatives interact with the linear changes of variables as usual, according to the chain rule;

•

The Leibnitz rule for partial derivatives hods;

•

For
F,F^{\prime}
as in Notation
2.25

F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)F^{\prime}\left(\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)=F^{\prime}\left(\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)

(2.27)

•

Let
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. Take

F_{j}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j-1}}\right)\right),j=1,\ldots,n

F^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}_{j}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}_{j-1}}\right)\right),j=1,\ldots,n^{\prime},

where
m_{0\ldots n},m^{\prime}_{0\ldots n^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then

\left(\int\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j}\left(\bm{x}_{j-1}|\bm{x}_{j}\right)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}d^{m_{j}}\bm{x}_{j}\right)\left(\int\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}F^{\prime}_{j}\left(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j-1}|\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j}\right)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}-1}d^{m^{\prime}_{j}}\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j}\right)=

(2.28)

\int\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j}\left(\bm{x}_{j-1}|\bm{x}_{j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}F^{\prime}_{j}\left(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j-1}|\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}d^{m_{j}}\bm{x}_{j}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}d^{m^{\prime}_{j}}\bm{x}^{\prime}_{j}\right),

\bm{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{0}},\quad\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{n}}

Proof.

Most of the statements are trivial, so we only mark the way of the proof for some of the claims. The Leibnitz rule for the defining form of symbolic expression (
2.26
) is trivial by definition of the partial derivatives. To deal with tensor products use

\partial^{(a,b)}=\partial^{a}\otimes\partial^{b},

where
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m^{\prime}}
are two multiindices and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m+m^{\prime}}
is their concatenation (
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) and the
Remark
2.14
.

The commutativity (
2.27
) is due to Remark
2.12
. The relation (
2.28
) holds By Remark
2.14
.
∎

It is natural to write the symbolic kernel of
\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) as
24
24

24

It can be also considered as a special case of Notation
2.32

\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=\delta^{(n)}(\bm{x}-\bm{y}),\,\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then we can introduce the folowing formal rules.

Notation 2.28
.

\int\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)F\left(\bm{x},\bm{u}|\bm{z}\right)d^{n}\bm{x}=d^{n}\bm{x}=F\left(\bm{y},\bm{u}|\bm{z}\right),

and

\int F^{\prime}\left(\bm{u}|\bm{y},\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)d^{n}\bm{x}=d^{n}\bm{x}=F\left(\bm{u}|\bm{z},\bm{x}\right),

for appropriately chosen
F
and
F^{\prime}
. We assume that such an integral can be always separated from the long symbolic expression by “Fubini theorem” and that the factors with independent variables commute as in Remark
2.27
.

Example 2.29
.

By Remark
2.13
,for
m,m^{\prime},n^{\prime},n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
, and
G\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}+k}\right)\right)
we have

\mathfrak{A}_{{m+1,\ldots,m+m^{\prime}}}^{n+1,\ldots,n+m^{\prime}}F\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,n}^{1,\ldots,n}G=(F\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right))})\circ(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}\otimes G),

so

(\mathfrak{A}_{{m+1,\ldots,m+m^{\prime}}}^{n+1,\ldots,n+m^{\prime}}F\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,n}^{1,\ldots,n}G)\left(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)=

(2.29)

\int F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}^{\prime\prime},\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right))}\left(\bm{y}^{\prime}|\bm{y}^{\prime\prime}\right)G\left(\bm{y}^{\prime\prime},\bm{z}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))}\left(\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}|\bm{x}\right)d^{k}\bm{z}d^{m^{\prime}}\bm{y}^{\prime\prime}d^{n}\bm{x}^{\prime}=

\int F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}^{\prime},\bm{z}\right)G\left(\bm{y}^{\prime},\bm{z}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)d^{k}\bm{z},

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}.

Finally, to restore all the power of the standard symbolic notation for distributions, we have to allow formal transformations under the integral sign.

Notation 2.30
.

Let
n,m,r,k,m_{1\ldots k},m^{\prime}_{1\ldots k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

F_{j}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}_{j}}\right)\right),\,x_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+r},\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}})\,y_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n+m+r},\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}_{j}})

and
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
. We write

F\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=\int{\prod_{j=1}^{k}F_{j}\left(y_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})|x_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})\right)d^{k}\bm{z}}

if for general
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
the expression

\int{\prod_{j=1}^{k}F_{j}\left(y_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})|x_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z})\right)f(\bm{x})d^{k}\bm{z}d^{n}\bm{x}}

can be transformed to the one, translating to
F[f](\bm{y})
by Notations
2.24
,
2.25
, and
2.28
using

•

Formal linear invertible transformation of integration variables,

•

Formal integration by parts.

It is easy to see that the necessary change of variables is essentially unique (up to irrelevant renaming), so the construction above is well-defined. The “Fubini theorem” and properties stated in Remark
2.27
are still valid and can be extended to new operations, linear transformation of the distributional arguments and partial derivatives with respect to them.

Remark 2.31
.

Note that the order of formal kernels in the product is still not arbitrary: after all changes of variables it should be presented in the form (
2.26
), where, in particular, each integration variable appears first (if we are counting from left to right) as a distributional argument, then as a parameter. Notations
2.25
and
2.28
do not affect this rule. One may show that if the order can be changed so that the conditions of Notation
2.30
are preserved, than the resulting operator does not change
25
25

25

Roughly speaking, such a reordering is possible only for tensor products, for which the commutativity was already found in Remark
2.27
. In this light, we may postulate that the products of symbolic kernels is commutative, assuming that expression is defined if it can be reordered so that conditions of Notation
2.30
are satisfied. However, to avoid possible confusions, we will rarely use this possibility.

The restrictions can be naturally incorporated into this calculus.

Notation 2.32
.

For
n,m,k
let
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+k}\right))
and
G=\mathfrak{I}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}F
(or equivalently
F=\mathfrak{R}_{1,\ldots,k}^{1,\ldots,k}G
). Then

G(\bm{x},\bm{y}|\bm{z})=F(\bm{y}|\bm{x},\bm{z}),\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

However, this identification makes the notation even more confusing, so we will use it only is selected situations.
In particular, Notation
2.32
and (
2.23
)

\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}_{n}(\bm{x},\bm{k})=e^{i\eta\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}.

which makes the standard integral notation for the (partial) Fourier transform of distributions into a special case of (
2.13
).

Remark 2.33
.

In some cases it is convenient to go further and identify the formal symbol with a possibly discontinuous function making (
2.25
) precise. For example, we write

J_{+}\left(t|t^{\prime}\right)=\theta(t-t^{\prime}),\,J_{-}\left(t|t^{\prime}\right)=\theta(t^{\prime}-t),

J\left(t_{1},t_{2}|t^{\prime}\right)=\theta(t-t_{1})\theta(t_{2}-t)-\theta(t-t_{2})\theta(t_{1}-t),

and treat the Heaviside function just as
\theta\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that we always are interested in
L^{\infty}
class of such discontinuous kernels only. This identification, unlike the one in Notation
2.32
, is ill-behaved if the derivatives are involved. We also point out that in this form it is absolutely not clear that
J_{\pm}
and
J
are smooth with respect to its parameters, although from direct definitions (
2.16
-
2.18
) we know that they are.

Finally, we also use another intuitive notation for the integral operators of Subsection
2.7
, writing, for example,

\int_{-\infty}^{t}(\ldots)dt^{\prime}
instead of
\int J_{+}\left(t|t^{\prime}\right)(\ldots)dt^{\prime}
and similarly for
J
,
J_{-}
,
J^{(n)}{}
and
J^{(n)_{\pm}}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
).

To sum up, we see that all the operations on operators we have introduced have a simple intuitive presentation in the language of symbolic integrals. Most of the formal manipulations with such integrals are allowed by the facts we have established. However, overuse of this notation may hide under cover some non-trivial operations, so we often provide at least one precise translation for the formal notation used to avoid any confusion.

3
The domain
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
and the secondary quantization

3.1
Construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}

To begin with, we define the space of
l
-particle states.
We start from making the space
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right)
into a pre-Hilbert space with the scalar product

(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\int\overline{\Psi\left(\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}\right)}\Psi^{\prime}\left(\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}\right)d^{3l}\vec{p}_{1\ldots l},\,\forall\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right).

(3.1)

Remark 3.1
.

In the Lorentz invariant theories a
\frac{1}{2\omega_{0}(\vec{k})}

multiplier
26
26

26

Here we
\omega_{0}
is the dispersion function defined below.
is often added to the measure of integration to make the measure Lorentz invariant (sometimes together with a power of
2\pi
for convenience). Here we do not assume the Lorentz invariance and chose the simplest possible measure of integration. Note, that as long as the dispersion function
\omega_{0}
is as in Definition
1.2
, such a factor can be always absorbed into a redefinition of the wave function, without leaving the
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right)
class.

Remark 3.2
.

|(\Psi,\Psi)|\leq C||\Psi||_{\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),k,0}^{2}

for sufficiently large
C
and
k
. Thus the tautological map from
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right)
(with the standard topology) to the homonymous pre-Hilbert space is continuous.

Now we take into account the bosonic statistics and define the

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right)

and

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}={\bigoplus_{l=0}^{\infty}}_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}.

(3.2)

For
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
we denote by
\Psi_{l}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}
its
l
th component.
The scalar product on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
is given by

(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(\Psi_{l},\Psi^{\prime}_{l}),\,\forall\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}.

The completion of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
with respect to that product is the Hilbert space of physical states

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}.

Remark 3.3
.

The space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}
is important for the physical interpretation of the Hamiltonian perturbative QFT construction in Section
4
. It is also relevant for the conjugated operator notion (Definition
3.10
) and in particular for the unitarity condition in Subsection
4.2
. Besides these two contexts, we ignore the pre-Hilbert space structure and use instead the topology of the summands of (
3.2
) inherited from
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right)
.

It is convenient to introduce the truncated spaces

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\leq l}={\bigoplus_{l^{\prime}=0}^{l}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}.

We also define the vacuum vector as

\Omega_{0}=1\in\mathbb{C}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}.

Clearly,

(\Omega_{0},\Omega_{0})=1.

3.2
Operators on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}

As
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
are topological spaces, the space of operators
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
can be defined as usual. The following characterization of it will be convenient for us later.

Remark 3.4
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
and the set

\left\{A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right))|\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]=A\right\}=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right))\right],

where for
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})

\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\circ A\circ\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}.

Proof.

The correspondence is almost tautological.
If
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right))
satisfies
\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]=A
, then it is valued in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}
and in particular its restriction to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}\right)
. Conversely, if
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}\right)
, then we set
A=A^{\prime}\circ\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right)\right)
and get
\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]=A
. It is trivial to see that these two maps are inverse to each other.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

The algebra
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
consists of all operators
A:\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there is
l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

A_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\leq l^{\prime}}\right).

Here
A_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}}
is a restriction of
A
to the space
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}
.

Remark 3.6
.

One may show that the notation
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
becomes precise if we endow
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
with the final topology induced by the inclusions
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\leq l}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Still, the direct characterization above is more convenient.

Remark 3.7
.

All elements of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
are at the same time densely-defined (unbounded) operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}
. Their domain of definition is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
, and they leave it invariant.

Definition 3.8
.

The space
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})

is defined by

\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})={\bigoplus_{l,l^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}}_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})

Remark 3.9
.

There is a natural way to embed
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
into
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
as an ideal, but we do not need that. It serves as a space of “unquantized” operators on which the second quantization operation (see Section
3.4
) is defined.

In the context of Remark
3.7
, it seems natural to look for the adjoint operators
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})^{*}
. For obvious reasons,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})^{*}
is not a subspace of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
, and furthermore, elements of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})^{*}
could be not defined on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
at all.
So, we introduce an alternative operation on
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
.

Definition 3.10
.

Fix an operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
. We say that it
has a conjugate operator
if there is
A^{{\dagger}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
such that

(\Psi^{\prime},A\Psi)=(A^{{\dagger}}\Psi^{\prime},\Psi),\quad\forall\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}.

Remark 3.11
.

If
A
has a conjugate, then by definition
A^{{\dagger}}\subset A^{*}
is the restriction of the adjoint operator to the space
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
. In general.
The operators satisfying
A^{{\dagger}}=A
are exactly the symmetric operators defined on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Remark 3.12
.

If
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
have conjugates, then so does
A\circ B
, and, moreover,
(A\otimes B)^{{\dagger}}=B^{{\dagger}}\otimes A^{{\dagger}}

Remark 3.13
.

With almost no changes Definition can be applied for
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
and
A^{{\dagger}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l})
for some
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
or to the case then both belong to
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
. We use this generalization.

3.3
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
and
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
-valued functions and distributions

Lead by the ideas of Section
2
, we introduce the following notions.

Definition 3.14
.

Fix
n,l,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
The space of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(l)}
-valued functions of class
\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
is

Remark 3.22
.

(\underline{F}\underline{F^{\prime}})[f\otimes f^{\prime}]=\underline{F}[f]\circ\underline{F^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}],\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right).

Remark 3.24
.

As was established in Section
2
the correspondences behind Notation
3.19
and Remark
3.20
are one-to-one. In particular, it means that the “underlining” is an invertible operation.
This allows to define the operators via symbolic expressions with their underlined counterparts. For example, for
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
and
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
we may write

\underline{\Phi}(\bm{y})=\int\underline{F}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)\underline{\Psi}(\bm{x})d^{n}\bm{x},\,\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

instead of
\Phi=F\circ\Psi
. We will see more complicated examples in which, unlike the trivial one above, the symbolic notation is more insightful.

In the light of the last remark, the following definition is well formulated.

Definition 3.25
.

For
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
we set
A^{{\dagger}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
to be a unique operator, characterized by

\underline{A^{{\dagger}}}[f](\bm{x})=\underline{A}[\overline{f}](\bm{x})^{{\dagger}},\,\forall f\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+k}.

(if it exists).

Remark 3.26
.

Remark
3.12
does not generalize to this setting directly, because the operators, in general, appear in incompatible order. However, the following is true:

•

If
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{l}\right))
have conjugated operators (
n,m,k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) and

C=A\widehat{\otimes}B,

then
C
has a conjugated operator, defined by

\underline{C}\left(\bm{y},\bm{y^{\prime}}|\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)=\underline{B}^{{\dagger}}\left(\bm{y^{\prime}}|\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)\underline{A}^{{\dagger}}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right),\,\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\bm{y^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}.

•

If
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))

A
has a conjugated operator, that so does
F\circ A
. Moreover,
(F\circ A)^{{\dagger}}=\overline{F}\circ A^{{\dagger}}
. Here
\overline{F}
is a unique operator in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
such that

\overline{F}[f]=\overline{F[\overline{f}]},\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

The proof is straightforward. It is convenient to use Remark
2.11
.

3.4
Secondary quantization

In perturbative quantum field theory we deal with a very special kind of operators in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
(and thus very special kinds of operator-valued functions and distributions). The relevant operators are constructed by smearing tensor (or Wick) products of the creation and annihilation operator-valued distributions with some test functions and, more generally, with distributions with particular types of singularities (see e.g.
[
19
]
). We instead first characterize the explicitly by their action on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
and then show correspondence with more traditional approaches.

We use the name second quantization for this procedure resembles the way how the many-particle operators (say the energy and the momentum) are constructed from the single-particle ones in the second quantization in physical literature. We first present the construction in details for the operators and then explain how it generalizes to operator-valued functions and distributions (including parameter-dependent ones).

3.4.1
Basic construction

Definition 3.27
.

Let
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
. The
second quantization
of
A
is
\widehat{A}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
defined by

\widehat{A}\Psi=\frac{\sqrt{n!(n-l+l^{\prime})!}}{(n-l)!}\left(\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\circ\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l+3l^{\prime}}A\right)\right)\Psi,\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n},\qquad n\geq l.

(3.3)

\widehat{A}\Psi=0,\qquad\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n},\qquad n<l.

Remark 3.28
.

Recall that

\mathfrak{A}_{3l+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l+3l^{\prime}}A=A\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(n-l)}\right))}.

This form is less direct but more intuitive.

Remark 3.29
.

To understand the meaning of the rather technical Definition let us introduce, a bit in advance, the standard creation and annihilation operator-valued distributions which we denote with
\underline{\widehat{a}_{+}}
and
\underline{\widehat{a}_{-}^{R}}
as they will appear in Examples
3.43
and
3.44

30
30

30

We use a non-Lorentz-invariant normalization leading to the commutation relation (
3.7
).
. Then Definition
3.27
is a formalization of
31
31

31

See Example
3.49
for the rigorous meaning of (
3.4
)

\widehat{A}=\int\left(\prod_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\underline{\widehat{a}_{+}}(\vec{p}_{j}^{\prime})\right)A\left(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{l}\underline{\widehat{a}_{-}^{R}}(\vec{p}_{j})\right)d^{3l}\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}d^{3l^{\prime}}\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}}

(3.4)

Here we took into account that the annihilation operator is in fact a smooth function of its parameter (in our terminology it means that it has a restriction which we denoted with
a_{-}^{R}
). The correspondence becomes straightforward in the case then
A
has a smooth kernel (in our terminology, A has an
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right)
-class restriction with respect to all its distributional arguments). Then coincidence of (
3.4
) and (
3.3
) follows by comparison of their value on generic
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
. The operators we consider in this paper are of this type only, but for the operator-valued distributions defined in the next subsection the general construction of Definition (
3.3
) is more convenient. It is worth noting that operators with a smooth kernel could never realize momentum or energy conservation law, so in the context of the strong adiabatic limit the full construction will be necessary.

Remark 3.30
.

The map
A\mapsto\widehat{A}
is obviously linear. It has a straightforward linear extension to
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
which we assume to be defined from now on.

Remark 3.31
.

We can extend the secondary quantization from the space
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
to
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right)\right)
keeping (
3.3
) untouched. It is easy that
\widehat{A}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]}
. Our choice is dictated by injectivity of the second quantization acting defined on
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
32
32

32

To see injectivity of second quantization on
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}\right)
consider its action on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}
. For the whole space
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
, proceed by induction in
l
.
.

The main advantage of this formalism is the fact that all operations on such operators can be done on the level of
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
without keeping track of the extra variables and combinatoric factors. In particular, the conjugation and the products can be computed that way.

Remark 3.32
.

For any
A\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
one has

\widehat{A^{{\dagger}}}=\widehat{A}^{{\dagger}}.

In other words, second quantization commutes with conjugation.

Proof.

It is enough to consider
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
for all possible
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and show for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
n\geq l
, any
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n}
and
\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n-l+l^{\prime}}
that

(\Psi^{\prime},\mathfrak{A}_{l+1,\ldots,n}^{l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,n-l+l^{\prime}}A\Psi)={(\mathfrak{A}_{l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,n-l+l^{\prime}}^{l+1,\ldots,n}A^{{\dagger}}\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})}.

Note that the combinatoric factors canceled out and we put away the symmetrization, for both
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}
being symmetric already. The last line is equivalent to

\int\left(\mathfrak{e}_{3l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3(n-l+l^{\prime})}^{n-l+l^{\prime}}\Psi^{\prime}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l}),A\mathfrak{e}_{3l+1,\ldots,3n}^{n}\Psi(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l})\right)d^{3(n-l)}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l}=

\int\left(A^{{\dagger}}\mathfrak{e}_{3l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3(n-l+l^{\prime})}^{n-l+l^{\prime}}\Psi^{\prime}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l}),\mathfrak{e}_{3l+1,\ldots,3n}^{n}\Psi(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l})\right)d^{3(n-l)}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n-l},

which holds by Definition
3.10
.
∎

Let us now compute the product
\widehat{A}\widehat{B}
for some
A,B\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
. First of all, note that the creation and operators in (
3.4
) are normally ordered. Then to present
\widehat{A}\widehat{B}
again in the form (
3.4
) we need some kind of the Wick theorem. So, we need a formalization of the Wick product with contractions. Looking once again on (
3.4
) we identify the tensor product in
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
with the Wick product:

:\widehat{A}\widehat{B}:=\widehat{A\otimes B}.

Inspired by that we introduce the following

Definition 3.33
.

Let

A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}},DS^{l^{\prime}_{A}})
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{B}},DS^{l^{\prime}_{B}})
and let
r\leq\min(l_{A},l^{\prime}_{B})
. Then the
tensor product with
r
contractions

A\otimes_{r}B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}+l_{B}-r},DS^{l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r})

is

A\otimes_{r}B=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left[(\mathfrak{A}_{3(l_{A}-r)+1,\ldots,3(l_{A}^{\prime}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r)}^{3(l^{\prime}_{A}-r)+1,\ldots,3(l^{\prime}_{A}-l_{B}+r)}A)\circ{}(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,3(l_{A}-r)}^{1,\ldots,3(l_{A}-r)}B)\right].

Remark 3.34
.

For clarity, we provide more readable versions of Definition
3.33
. First of all, reading the proof of Proposition
3.35
it may be convenient to keep in mind that

A\otimes_{r}B=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left[\left(A\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l^{\prime}_{B}-r)}\right)\right)}\right)\left(1_{\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l_{A}-r)}\right)\right)}\otimes B\right)\right].

To see that this is indeed the Wick product with
r
contractions, the symbolic form is more suitable. For the sake of clarity, we omit the symmetrization operator which is not relevant by Remark
3.31
.

A\otimes_{r}B=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left[A\otimes^{\prime}_{r}B\right],

\left(A\otimes^{\prime}_{r}B\right)\left(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots l_{A}+l_{B}-r}\right)=\int A\left(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}_{A}}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots l_{A}-r},\vec{k}_{1\ldots r}\right)\times

B\left(\vec{k}_{1\ldots r},\vec{p}^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}_{A}+1\ldots l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r}|\vec{p}_{l_{A}-r+1\ldots l_{A}+l_{B}-r}\right)d^{3r}\vec{k}_{1\ldots r}.

Comparing it with (
3.4
) we see that our terminology is natural. Finally, such a product may be presented graphically as in Fig.
1
. Here each dot denotes an operator and each line incident to it is an argument of the corresponding operator, distributional (for lines coming from the right), or parametric (for the lines going to the left). The arguments, connecting two dots are subject to integration. Note that the graph is totally ordered
33
33

33

see Subsection
1.1
for terminology related to the Feynman graphs
. Clearly, consequent products may be given such representation in terms of totally ordered graphs.

A
B
\vdots
l^{\prime}_{A}
\vdots
l_{A}-r
\iddots
r
\vdots
l^{\prime}_{B}-r
\vdots
l_{B}

Figure 1:
Graphical presentation of the contracted product
A\otimes_{r}B\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}+l_{B}-r},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r})
,
A\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{A}})
,
B\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{B}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{B}})
.

Proposition 3.35
.

Let

A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{A}})
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{B}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{B}})
. Then

\widehat{A}\widehat{B}=\widehat{A\otimes_{\bullet}B}
with

A\otimes_{\bullet}B=\sum_{r=0}^{\min(l_{A},l^{\prime}_{B})}r!\binom{l_{A}}{r}\binom{l^{\prime}_{B}}{r}A\otimes_{r}B\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}).

Remark 3.36
.

The binomial coefficients are nothing but the number of selecting
r
pairs from the creation operators of
B
and annihilation operators of
A
, so the above is indeed a form of the Wick theorem.

The proof of Proposition
3.35
is straightforward, but technical. It is convenient to separate the following combinatoric facts.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
n,l_{A},l_{B},l_{A}^{\prime},l_{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
n\geq\min(l_{A},l_{A}+l_{B}-l_{B}^{\prime})
. Introduce

R:\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
as

R(\sigma)=\left|\{j=1,\ldots,l_{B}^{\prime}|\sigma(j)\leq l_{A}\}\right|.

For each
r\in\{\max(0,l_{A}+l_{B}-n),\max(0,l_{A}+l_{B}-n)+1,\ldots,\min(l_{A},l_{B}^{\prime})\}
define
\sigma^{(r)}
by setting

\sigma^{(r)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}}
as

\sigma^{(r)}(j)=j+l_{A}-r,\qquad 1\leq j\leq l_{B}^{\prime},

\sigma^{(r)}(j)=j-l_{B}^{\prime},\qquad l_{B}^{\prime}<j\leq l_{A}+l_{B}^{\prime}-r,

\sigma^{(r)}(j)=j,\qquad l_{A}+l_{B}^{\prime}-r<j\leq n.

Then

1.

\max(0,l_{A}+l_{B}-n)\leq R(\sigma)\leq\min(l_{A},l_{B}^{\prime})

\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}}
;

2.

R(\sigma^{(r)})=r
,
r\in\{\max(0,l_{A}+l_{B}-n),\ldots,\min(l_{A},l_{B}^{\prime})\}

3.

For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}}
there is a (not necessary unique) decomposition

\sigma=(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2})\circ\sigma^{(R(\sigma))}\circ(\sigma_{3}\times\sigma_{4})

(3.5)

with
\sigma_{1}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l_{A}}
,
\sigma_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{A}}
,
\sigma_{3}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l_{B}^{\prime}}
,
\sigma_{4}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}}
,
Here we assume the natural inclusion
\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k^{\prime}}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{k+k^{\prime}}
with the first and second factors acting, respectively, on the first
k
the rest
k^{\prime}
elements;

4.

\big|\{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}}|R(\sigma)=r\}\big|=\binom{l_{B}^{\prime}}{r}\binom{l_{A}}{r}r!\frac{(n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}-l_{A})!(n-l_{B})!}{(n-l_{B}-l_{A}+r)!}.

(3.6)

Proof.

1.

The upper bound
R(\sigma)\leq\min(l_{A},l_{B}^{\prime})
follows directly from the definition. At the same time

l_{B}^{\prime}-R(\sigma)=\left|\{j=1,\ldots,l_{B}^{\prime}|l_{A}+1\leq\sigma(j)\leq n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}\}\right|,

so
l_{B}^{\prime}-R(\sigma)\leq n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{A}
giving the desired lower bound.

2.

Directly by construction of
\sigma^{(r)}

\{j=1,\ldots,l_{B}^{\prime}|\sigma(j)\leq l_{A}\}=\{1,\ldots,r\},

so
R(\sigma^{(r)})=r
.

3,4

Let us classify all permutations
\sigma
with fixed
R(\sigma)=r
. To fix such a permutation we do the following:

•

We select
r
elements among the first
l_{B}^{\prime}
numbers, find for them places among the first
l_{A}
numbers, and chose a bijection between the former and the latter. This gives

\binom{l_{B}^{\prime}}{r}\binom{l_{A}}{r}r!

possibilities;

•

The rest
l_{B}^{\prime}-r
numbers from
1
to
l_{B}^{\prime}
should be placed somewhere from
l_{A}+1
to
n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}
leading to

\frac{(n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}-l_{A})!}{(n-l_{B}-l_{A}+r)!}

possibilities;

•

Finally, we should fix a bijection between the numbers from
l_{B}^{\prime}+1
to
n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}
and the yet free places in

(n-l_{B})!

ways.

The product of these factor give (
3.6
), and the described procedure gives a decomposition of type (
3.5
).

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.35
.

Take first
n<\max(l_{B},l_{A}+l_{B}-l^{\prime}_{B})
and
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n}
. We get
\widehat{A}\widehat{B}\Psi=0
immediately as well as
\widehat{A\otimes_{r}B}\Psi
for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,\min(l_{A},l^{\prime}_{B})\}
.

Now take
n\geq\max(l_{B},l_{A}+l_{B}-l^{\prime}_{B})
and again
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n}
.
We have

\widehat{A}\widehat{B}\Psi=\frac{\sqrt{n!(n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B})!}}{(n-l_{B})!}\frac{\sqrt{(n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B})!(n-l_{A}+l^{\prime}_{A}-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B})!}}{(n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}-l_{A})!}\times

\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+1,\ldots,3n_{A}-3(l_{A}+l_{B})+3(l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B})}A\right)\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)\Psi.

Now we present the operator
\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}
in the middle explicitly:

\widehat{A}\widehat{B}\Psi=c\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-l_{B}+l_{B}^{\prime}}}\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+1,\ldots,3n_{A}-3(l_{A}+l_{B})+3(l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B})}A\right)\circ

\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma]\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)\Psi,

c=\frac{\sqrt{n!(n-l_{A}+l^{\prime}_{A}-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B})!}}{(n-l_{B})!(n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}-l_{A})!}.

We apply Lemma
3.37
to classify the permutations. By the decomposition
3.5

\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma]=(\mathfrak{p}_{l_{A}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{1}]\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}-l_{A}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{2}])\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma^{(R(\sigma))}](\mathfrak{p}_{l_{B}^{\prime}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{3}]\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{4}]).

Since
\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[B]=B
and
\Psi=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\Psi
we get
34
34

34

We use that the symmetrization operator absorbs any permutations.
,

(\mathfrak{p}_{l_{B}^{\prime}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{3}]\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{4}])\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)\Psi=

\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)(\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,3l_{B}}^{1,\ldots,3l_{B}}\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{4}])\Psi=\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)Psi.

Similarly, using
\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}[A]=A
we get

\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+1,\ldots,3n_{A}-3(l_{A}+l_{B})+3(l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B})}A\right)(\mathfrak{p}_{l_{A}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{1}]\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}-l_{A}}^{*(3)}[\sigma_{2}])=

\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+1,\ldots,3n_{A}-3(l_{A}+l_{B})+3(l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B})}A\right)

Finally,

\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+1,\ldots,3n_{A}-3(l_{A}+l_{B})+3(l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B})}A\right)\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{B}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}B\right)=

\mathfrak{A}_{3l_{A}+3l_{B}+1,\ldots,3n}^{3l^{\prime}_{A}+3l_{B}^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3n-3l_{A}-3l_{B}+3l^{\prime}_{A}+3l_{B}^{\prime}}\left(A\otimes_{r}B\right).

Putting this all back and using
\widehat{A\otimes_{r}B}\Psi=0
whenever
n<l_{A}+l_{B}-r
we get

\widehat{A}\widehat{B}\Psi=\sum_{r=0}^{\min(l_{A},l_{B}^{\prime})}C_{r}\widehat{A\otimes_{r}B}\Psi,

where

C_{r}=c\big|\{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}}|R(\sigma)=r\}\big|\left(\frac{\sqrt{n!(n-l_{A}+l^{\prime}_{A}-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B})!}}{(n-l_{A}-l_{B}+r)!}\right)^{-1}=

\big|\{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+l_{B}^{\prime}-l_{B}}|R(\sigma)=r\}\big|\frac{(n-l_{A}-l_{B}+r)!}{(n-l_{B})!(n-l_{B}+l^{\prime}_{B}-l_{A})!}.

Thus by the last assertion of Lemma
3.37

C_{r}=\binom{l_{B}^{\prime}}{r}\binom{l_{A}}{r}r!
as in the statement.
∎

Remark 3.38
.

We can consider
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
as a formal involutive algebra with the product
\otimes_{\bullet}
. Then by Proposition
3.35
and Remark
3.32
the second quantization is a homomorphism of such algebras. On the other hand, it means that the whole analysis (see also Remark
3.50
) of this paper can be done completely within the formal algebra, without mentioning its particular representation by unbounded operators on the physical Hilbert space (similar to perturbative AQFT
[
19
]
).

3.4.2
Generalization to operator-valued functions and (possibly parameter-dependent) distributions

From this and the previous subsections it is clear that it is enough to define the secondary quantization for parameter-dependent operator-valued distribution as a map

\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))

for
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Then the case of operator-valued functions follows by setting
n=0
, while parameterless distributions correspond to
m=k=0
.

Generalization of Definition
3.27
is straightforward:

Definition 3.39
.

Let
n,m,k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
. The
second quantization
of
A
is
\widehat{A}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
defined by

\widehat{A}\Psi=\frac{\sqrt{L!(L-l+l^{\prime})!}}{(L-l)!}\left(\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3L+1,\ldots,3L+m+k}^{3L+1,\ldots,3L+m+k}\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\right)\circ\left(\mathfrak{A}_{3l+1,\ldots,3L}^{3l^{\prime}+1,\ldots,3L-3l+3l^{\prime}}A\right)\right)\Psi,

\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{L}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\qquad L\geq l;

\widehat{A}\Psi=0,\qquad\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{L}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\qquad L<l.

Remark 3.40
.

By Remark
2.14
,
the
secondary quantization commutes with evaluation
, i.e. for
A
as in Definition
3.39

\widehat{\underline{A}[f](\bm{x})}=\underline{\widehat{A}}[f](\bm{x}),\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+k}.

As a consequence,

\widehat{A}^{{\dagger}}=\widehat{A^{{\dagger}}}.

Remark 3.41

(Wick’s theorem for functions and distributions)
.

Take
l_{A},l^{\prime}_{A},l_{B},l^{\prime}_{B},n,n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
A\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{A}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right)
and
B\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{B}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{B}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right)\right)
. Then Proposition
3.35
holds in the same symbolic form with

A\otimes_{r}B\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l_{A}+l_{B}-r}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}_{A}+l^{\prime}_{B}-r}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right)

defined precisely as in Definition
3.33
. The proof goes along the same lines. Generalization to the multiplier and mixed spaces goes along the lines of Subsection
2.6
.

Remark 3.42
.

Let
n,m,k,r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
A\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
, and two tuples of pairwise distinct numbers
i_{1\ldots r}\in\{1,\ldots,n+r\}
and
i^{\prime}_{1\ldots r}\in\{1,\ldots,m+k+r\}
. Define

B=\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{i_{1\ldots r}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots r}}A
.
Then, by Remark
2.14
,

\widehat{B}=\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{i_{1\ldots r}}^{i^{\prime}_{1\ldots r}}\widehat{A}.

In other word,
secondary quantization commutes with augmentations
.

Further properties of the secondary quantization and its generalization will be stated after some examples.

3.4.3
Important examples

We list a few applications of the formalism introduced in this subsection. On the one hand, they illustrate the framework we have constructed. On the other hand, they play important roles in what follows.

Example 3.43
.

Define
35
35

35

Here we use
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)=\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
. Simple identifications appear in other examples with no further comments.

a_{+}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right))

a_{+}[f](\vec{p})=f(\vec{p})

and

a_{-}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathbb{C})
,

a_{-}[f]=\int{f(\vec{p},\vec{p})d^{3}{\vec{p}}}.

Then
a_{-}=a_{+}^{{\dagger}}
, thus
\widehat{a}_{-}=\widehat{a}_{+}^{{\dagger}}
. The operator-valued distributions

\underline{\widehat{a}_{\pm}}

coincide with the the standard creation and annihilation distributions. By Wick’s theorem of Remark
3.41
(or by direct computation),

\underline{\widehat{a_{-}}}(\vec{p})\underline{\widehat{a_{+}}}(\vec{p}^{\prime})-\underline{\widehat{a_{+}}}(\vec{p}^{\prime})\underline{\widehat{a_{-}}}(\vec{p})=\delta^{(3)}(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\prime})\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})},

(3.7)

Where we treat
\delta^{(3)}(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\prime})
as a (symbolic kernel of a) distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{6}
.

Example 3.44
.

There is a restriction
a_{-}^{R}=\mathfrak{R}_{1,2,3}^{1,2,3}a_{-}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{3},\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right))
,

a_{-}^{R}[\psi](\vec{p})=\psi(\vec{p},\vec{p}),\,\forall\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1},\,\forall\vec{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

It has the secondary quantization
\widehat{a}^{R}_{-}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right))
and does not have the conjugate operator. This example is in agreement with the well-known fact that the annihilation (but not the creation) operator is in fact a smooth function of its parameter.

Remark 3.45
.

The commutation relations for
a_{-}^{R}
in place of
a_{-}
have the same form, but the delta-function should be understood as a parameter-dependent distribution, namely

\delta^{(3)}(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\prime})=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right))}

(see Notation
2.32
).

Example 3.46
.

Another class of examples comes from the identification

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)).

By Definition
3.39
, for
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right))
,

\widehat{F}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}}\otimes F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)).

It is consistent with Notation
3.21
.
The Wick Theorem of Remark
3.41
, applied to products with one of the multipliers of this class leads to rather trivial result (as the number of contraction is always zero). In particular
36
36

36

As usual, we put
k=0
for simplicity
,
for
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right))
,
A\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\right)
and
B\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)\right)
we get

F\otimes_{\bullet}A=F\otimes_{0}A=\widehat{F}\circ A\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\right)

and

B\otimes_{\bullet}F=B\otimes_{0}F=B\circ\widehat{F}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}\right)\right).

In the symbolic notation we write

\underline{\widehat{F\otimes_{\bullet}A}}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=\int F(\bm{z}|\bm{y})\underline{\widehat{A}}\left(\bm{z}|\bm{x}\right)d^{n}\bm{z},\quad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

and

\underline{\widehat{B\otimes_{\bullet}F}}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{x}\right)=\int\underline{\widehat{B}}\left(\bm{y}|\bm{z}\right)F\left(\bm{z}|\bm{x}\right)d^{m}\bm{z},\quad,\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\bm{y}\in\bm{m^{\prime}},

where we omit the hat on the symbolic kernel of the numerical quantization
F
(as in Notation
3.23
). The main lesson we learned here is that composition with (hatted) numerical operator as above can be pulled into the second quantization.

We are now ready to define the quantum fields.

Example 3.47
.

Let
\omega_{0}
be a massive dispersion function (Definition
1.2
).
We define
\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
as

\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0}=\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0+}+\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0-},

where
37
37

37

We use properties of
\omega_{0}
postulated in Definition
1.2

\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0\pm}\left(t|\vec{p}\right)=a_{\pm}(\pm\vec{p})\varphi_{\pm}(t,\vec{p}),

(3.8)

\varphi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right),\quad\varphi_{\pm}(\vec{p},t)=\frac{e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\omega_{0}(\vec{p})t}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{3}2\omega_{0}(\vec{p})}}\,(\forall\vec{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\,\forall t\in\mathbb{R}).

We also set
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}=\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}^{1}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}^{R}
.
Then

\underline{\widehat{\phi}}_{0,\pm}(t,\vec{k})=\underline{\widehat{\phi}}_{0,\pm}^{R}\left(t|\vec{k}\right)=\underline{\widehat{a}}_{\pm}(\vec{k})\frac{e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\omega_{0}(\vec{k})t}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{3}2\omega_{0}(\vec{k})}}.

which is nothing but the positive and the negative frequency parts of the partial Fourier transform of the real scalar quantum field.

Remark 3.48
.

For the completeness we present a translation of (
3.8
) from the symbolic language.
We take arbitrary
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
and write

\underline{\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0\pm}\circ f}=\int\underline{a}_{\pm}(\pm\vec{p})\varphi_{\pm}(t,\vec{p})\underline{f}(t,\vec{p})\psi dtd^{3}{\vec{p}}=\widetilde{\phi}_{0\pm}[f]=\int\underline{a}_{\pm}(\vec{p})\varphi_{\pm}(t,\pm\vec{p})\underline{f}(\pm\vec{p})dtd^{3}{\vec{p}}.

As
\varphi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
, we present multiplication by it as
\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi_{\pm}}\mathfrak{A}_{1,2,3}^{2,3,4}{\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right)}}
and add a hat according to Notation
3.23
. Treating the linear transform once again by Notations
2.25
and
3.23
we arrive to

\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{0\pm}=a_{\pm}\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}^{1}\widehat{\left(\pm\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\right)}\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi_{\pm}}}\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,2,3}^{2,3,4}{\widehat{\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right)}}}.

We also set the position space presentation for the quantum fields as
\phi_{0}^{R}=\widetilde{\phi_{0}^{R}}\circ\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{3}
and
\phi_{0}=\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}^{1}\phi_{0}^{R}
. Symbolically we have, for example,

\phi_{0}^{R}\left(\vec{x}|t\right)=\int e^{\mathrm{i}\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}^{R}(\vec{k})d^{3}\vec{k}.

Remark 3.49
.

With Examples
3.43
-
3.46
the expression (
3.4
) gets a precise sense. Its translation from the symbolic language for
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}})
(
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) is

\widehat{A}=\widehat{a}_{+}^{\widehat{\otimes}l^{\prime}}\circ\widehat{A\circ\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}}\circ\widehat{a}_{-}^{R\widehat{\otimes}l},

where
A\circ\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\in{\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}\right)\right)}
is a numerical distribution, so for
\widehat{A\circ\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}}
we can use Notation
3.21
.
Similar expression can be written for general Definition
3.39
.

Remark 3.50
.

The Wick’s theorem of Remark
3.41
may be derived from (
3.7
) by means of the symbolic calculus.
In this sense the spaces
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\right)
can be considered as formal spaces generated by
a_{+}
and
a_{-}^{R}
with the product
\otimes_{\bullet}
, the partially defined involution
{\dagger}
and operations of Notation
3.21
. As was anticipated in Remark
3.38
, this allows to forget about the particular realization of the elements of
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\right)
as parameter-dependent (unbounded) operator-valued distributions acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\rm{phys}}
and define Hamiltonian perturbative QFT (Section
4
) on the formal algebra language.

For future use we also define the Wick products. Note that the singular products never appear in the non-local quantum field theory, so we can survive with a very simple definition.

Example 3.51
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}
we set

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{a}}_{\alpha_{j}}(\vec{p}_{j}):=\underline{\widehat{a_{:\alpha_{1\ldots n}}:}}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}),

(3.9)

where
a_{:\alpha_{1\ldots n}:}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}{})
is uniquely defined by

a_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left[\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}\right]=\bm{\Sigma}^{(3)}\left[\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\underline{a_{\alpha_{j}}}[f_{j}]\right],\,\forall f_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(3.10)

To see that (
3.9
) indeed defines the Wick product, note that the tensor product is the same as product with no contructions. In particular, if
\alpha_{j}
are normally ordered (i.e. if
\alpha_{i}=+
and
\alpha_{j}=-
, then
i<j
), then

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{a}}_{\alpha_{j}}(\vec{p}_{j}):=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes}_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{a}}_{\alpha_{j}}\right)(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}),

and the other cases follow by obvious symmetry of (
3.10
). It is, of course, possible to present explicitly for each
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
action of such operator on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
, but we omit it to avoid unecessary complicated combinatorics.

Example 3.52
.

Continuing the previous example, we set

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}|\vec{p}_{j}\right):=\underline{\widehat{a_{:\alpha_{1\ldots n}}:}}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j},\vec{p}_{j}),

and

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}|\vec{p}_{j}\right):=\sum_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}=\pm}:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{R}_{0\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}|\vec{p}_{j}\right),

and

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{R}_{0\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}|\vec{x}_{j}\right):=\int:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}|\vec{p}_{j}\right)e^{\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{p}_{j}\cdot\vec{x}_{j}}d^{3n}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}.

Clearly, all these operators can be presented as second quantization of some objects in
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
.
The unrestricted form and the can be defined similarly.

4
Non-local Hamiltonian Perturbation Quantum Field Theory

4.1
Motivation: Hamiltonian Perturbation Quantum Field Theory

In order to motivate the technical constructions of this section and fix the terminology we briefly (and rather informally) recall how the Hamiltonian perturbation theory is usually constructed. A rigorous version of this construction for a class of non-local quantum field theories is presented in the next subsections.

The goal is to construct the operator-valued distributions
\phi
,
\pi
satisfying the commutation relations

[\phi(t,\vec{x}),\pi(t,\vec{x}^{\prime})]=-i\delta(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}).

(4.1)

and the equations of motion

\partial_{t}\phi(x)=-i[H(t),\phi(t,\vec{x})],

(4.2)

\partial_{t}\pi(x)=-i[H(t),\pi(t,\vec{x})].

(4.3)

Here the Hamiltonian operator
H(t)

H(t)=H_{0}(t)+H_{int}(t).

The first term, the free Hamiltonian
H_{0}
is chosen so that the solution of (
4.1
-
4.3
) for
H_{0}
in place of
H
is known. More precisely, we are given
\phi_{0}
,
\pi_{0}
such, that

[\phi_{0}(t,\vec{x}),\pi_{0}(t,\vec{x}^{\prime})]=-i\delta(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}).

(4.4)

\partial_{t}\phi_{0}(x)=-i[H(t),\phi_{0}(t,\vec{x})],

(4.5)

\partial_{t}\pi_{0}(x)=-i[H(t),\pi_{0}(t,\vec{x})].

(4.6)

The interaction part of the Hamiltonian
H_{int}(t)
has the form

H_{int}(t)=g\int{h_{int}(t,\vec{x})\lambda(t,\vec{x})}d^{3}\vec{x},

where
h_{i}nt(x)

is a fixed translationally-invariant polynomial functional of the field
\phi(t,\vec{x})
and its conjugated momentum
\pi(t,\vec{x})
. The function
\lambda(t,\vec{x})
is the adiabatic cut-off. As mentioned in Introduction, its presence is necessary due to the Haag theorem, forbidding the existence of the unitary equivalence between the free and the interacting theories (
4.7
) below for translationally-invariant interaction. At this moment we need
\lambda(t,\vec{x})
to decay then
t\rightarrow\infty
in order to make the integral in (
4.9
) below converge. In the next subsection we will see that
\lambda
should also decay whenever
\vec{x}\rightarrow\infty
for
H_{int}
to be a well-defined unbounded operator. The function
\lambda
should also be smooth enough to make the switching adiabatic. These conditions are guaranteed if we assume
\lambda\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
. To get physically relevant information we should pass to the limit
\lambda(t,\vec{x})\rightarrow 1
in the appropriate sense.
Finally,
g
is a formal parameter introduced for convenience.

Then we construct a formal solution of (
4.1
-
4.3
) in the form

\phi(\vec{x},t)=U(t)^{-1}\phi_{0}(\vec{x},t)U(t),

(4.7)

\pi(\vec{x},t)=U(t)^{-1}\pi_{0}(\vec{x},t)U(t),

(4.8)

where
U(t)
is given by

U(t)=\sum_{n}(-i)^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}\ldots\int_{-\infty}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}H_{I}(t_{j})d^{n}t_{1\ldots n}}=

(4.9)

{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{e^{-i\int_{\tau<t^{\prime}}{H_{I}(\tau)d\tau}}\right\},

where
{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{\ldots\right\}
is the symbolic time-ordering operator and

H_{I}(t)=U(t)H_{int}(t)U(t)^{-1}.

(4.10)

Assuming that
H_{int}
is symmetric, we may expect that
U(t)
is (in some sense) unitary.

We claim that (
4.7
-
4.8
) is a solution of (
4.1
-
4.3
). First, (
4.7
-
4.8
) together with (
4.4
) imply the commutation relations (
4.1
). To get the equations of motion we formally differentiate (
4.9
) to get

\frac{dU(t)}{dt}=-iH_{I}(t)U(t).

Now differentiating (
4.6
-
4.5
) and substituting (
4.10
)
we arrive to (
4.3
-
4.2
). Now let us assume for simplicity that
H_{int}(t)
is a polynomial functional of
\phi
and
\pi
at the time
t
. Then by (
4.7
-
4.8
) definition of
H_{I}
(
4.10
) may be rewritten as

H_{I}(t)=H_{int}(t)_{\phi\rightarrow\phi_{0},\hskip 8.19447pt\pi\rightarrow\pi_{0}}.

(4.11)

There are (at least) two ways to get physically relevant information from this construction. First of all, we may the scattering operator. For simplicity of the interpretation let us assume that
\lambda(y,\vec{x})
vanishes for
t>|T|
. Then the same holds for
H_{int}(t)
and thus
H_{I}(t)
. We have

U(t)=\bm{1},\quad t<-T;\qquad U(t)=S,\quad t>T

for some unitary operator
S
. Then from (
4.7
-
4.8
) we see that in the distant past the interacting field
\phi
coincides with the free one, while in the distant future it belongs to a unitary equivalent realization of the same commutation relations. These two realizations induce two interpretations of the Hilbert space as a Fock space, giving rise the states of incoming and the outgoing particles respectively. Then we can recognize the operator
S
, intertwining these two realizations, as the scattering operator. This object has a direct physical interpretation, but its adiabatic limit (see next subsection or the introduction for a discussion of the adiabatic limit) is rather delicate
[
9
]
. We postpone it for the next publication
[
22
]
.

Another type of objects of interest are the correlators. We consider two families of distributions
38
38

38

We use a non-standard partially Fourier transformed presentation of all functions for technical convenience.
:

•

The
Wightman functions

\mathcal{W}_{n}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

\mathcal{W}_{n}\left((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}\right)=\left(\Omega,\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}(\vec{t}_{j},\vec{p}_{j})\Omega\right)

(4.12)

•

The
Green functions

\mathcal{G}_{n}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

\mathcal{G}_{n}\left((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}\right)=\left(\Omega,{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}(\vec{t}_{j},\vec{p}_{j})\right\}\Omega\right).

(4.13)

Equations (
4.12
-
4.13
) are written already in the adiabatic limit (or at least the temporal adiabatic limit
\lambda(\vec{x},t)\rightarrow\lambda_{\mathrm{S}}(\vec{x})
), and
\Omega
is the vacuum state of the full Hamiltonian. To compute it in the frame of the perturbation theory one may use the Gell-Man and Low theorem
[
23
,
24
]
giving

\mathcal{W}_{n}=\lim_{\lambda(\vec{x},t)\rightarrow\lambda_{\mathrm{S}}(\vec{x})}\frac{W_{n}}{W_{0}},\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(4.14)

and

\mathcal{G}_{n}=\lim_{\lambda(\vec{x},t)\rightarrow\lambda_{\mathrm{S}}(\vec{x})}\frac{G_{n}}{G_{0}},\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(4.15)

where

W_{n}\left((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}\right)=\left(\Omega_{0},S\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}(t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})\Omega_{0}\right)=

(4.16)

\left(\Omega_{0},U(+\infty,t_{1})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}(t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})U(t_{j},t_{j+1})\Omega_{0}\right)

and

G_{n}\left((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}\right)=\left(\Omega_{0},S{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}(t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})\right\}\Omega_{0}\right)=

(4.17)

\left(\Omega_{0},{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}(t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})e^{-i\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{H_{I}(\tau)d\tau}}\right\}\Omega_{0}\right)

with
\Omega_{0}
being the vacuum vector of the free theory (in accordance with the one defined in Section
3
), assuming that the limits above exist. This limit is known as the weak adiabatic limit and is the central object of this paper. Comparing the weak and strong adiabatic limit existence theorems
[
10
,
9
]
we may expect that the former will be much easier to prove than the latter.

The LSZ procedure
39
39

39

The LSZ reduction for non-local theories was considered in
[
12
]
.
gives a connection between the strong and weak adiabatic limit, allowing to retrieve the matrix elements of
S
from the residues of the Fourier transform of the Green functions.

Before going further let us briefly summarize the unclarified issues of the exposition above.

Remark 4.1
.

The objects
\phi
and
\pi
(respectively
\phi_{0}
and
\pi_{0}
) satisfying (
4.1
) (respectively (
4.4
)) may exist only as distributions valued in unbounded operators. We build them as elements of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}{})
which has restrictions
40
40

40

Otherwise the simultaneous commutation relations can not be defined.
and the mentioned relations must be rewritten more carefully, as was done in Example
3.47
. In other words, they make sense then both hands sides are applied to a vector in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
with appropriate
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Remark 4.2
.

We deal with right-hand sides of (
4.9
) as with a formal power series in terms of the formal parameter
g
. Thus, in addition to the previous Remark, to make sense of all expressions containing
H
,
H_{int}
,
S
,
U
,
\phi
and
\pi
we have to truncate them at arbitrary finite order (see Subsection
1.1
).

Remark 4.3
.

To make sense of (
4.7
-
4.10
) and dependent equations we have to treat
t
as a parameter rather than a distributional argument. In Remark
4.1
we already noted that by writing (
4.1
-
4.6
) we have implicitly assumed the existence of restrictions of operator-valued distributions
\phi
,
\pi
,
\phi_{0}
and
\pi_{0}
to fixed values of
t
. We also have to require that
U
and
H_{I}
are functions of
t
. In local QFT, due to the UV singularities,
H_{I}
exists only as a distribution and thus all expressions including the time-ordered product require a regularization. This problem does not appear in the class of non-local quantum field theories we define in the rest of the section.

4.2
Admissible Quantum Field Theories

Here we define a class of admissible quantum field theories for which the construction of the previous subsection becomes precise.

4.2.1
Free Quantum Field and admissible dispersion relation

Instead of the fields themselves, in the non-local case it is more convenient to work with their partial Fourier transforms. The appropriate definition of such an object was already given in Example
3.47
. We use it always assuming the mass gap. Analogously, within the same notation we can define the momentum
\widetilde{\pi}_{0}
as

\pi_{0}(t,\vec{k})=\partial_{t}\phi_{0}(t,\vec{k}).

Using the Wick Theorem of Remark
3.41
we may show that

[\underline{\widehat{\phi}}(t,\vec{k}),\underline{\widehat{\pi}}(t,\vec{k}^{\prime})]=-i\widehat{\delta}(\vec{k}+\vec{k^{\prime}})\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})}

which is the precise form of the Fourier transform of (
4.4
).

4.2.2
Admissible interaction

The next ingredient is the interaction
H_{I}
. Our approach mostly follows
[
12
]
.

Definition 4.4
.

We say that
h_{I}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
is an
admissible interaction density
if for each
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

there is
F_{(l,l^{\prime})}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right)
, which we call
the admissible interaction kernels
such that

h_{I}[\psi]_{l^{\prime}}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},t)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\int{F^{\prime}_{(l^{\prime},l)}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})\widehat{\mathfrak{F}^{3}_{-}}\psi\left(\vec{p}_{1\ldots l},\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\vec{p^{\prime}}_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\vec{p}_{j}\right)}\times

(4.18)

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\omega_{0}(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\omega_{0}(\vec{p}_{j})\right)t\right)d^{3l}\vec{p}_{1\ldots l},

\forall\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{l^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\quad\forall\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}},\,\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

\left((\vec{p}_{1\ldots l},\vec{p}^{\prime}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}})\mapsto F_{(l,l^{\prime})}s\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\vec{p^{\prime}}_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\vec{p}_{j}\right)\right)\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right),\,\forall s\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right)

(4.19)

and

h_{I}^{{\dagger}}=h_{I}.

The following remarks clarify the technical definition above.

Remark 4.5
.

The condition (
4.19
) basically limits the directions in which
F_{(l,l^{\prime})}
can grow. We could instead change the variables of integration in (
4.18
) and ask the kernel to belong to the mixed space, but that would break the symmetry of Definition
4.4
.

Remark 4.6
.

An admissible interaction is characterized by its kernels. These kernels are constrained by the following.
By definition of
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}
there can be only finitely many non-zero kernels among
F_{(l^{\prime},l)}
. Each such kernel should be symmetric with respect to its first
l^{\prime}
arguments for
h_{I}
to value in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right)
. Symmetry in the next
l
arguments can be always assumed as we do from now on. Finally, the ”hermicity” condition translates to

F^{\prime}_{(l^{\prime},l)}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})=\overline{F^{\prime}_{(l,l^{\prime})}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots l},\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}})},\quad\forall l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,\forall\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}\mathbb{R}^{3l},\,\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3l^{\prime}}.

It is easy to see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the admissible interaction densities and the families of kernels satisfying all conditions listed here.

Remark 4.7
.

In the symbolic notation Definition
4.4
reads as
41
41

41

to make it precise we have to use presentation of
F_{l,l^{\prime}}
via
F^{\prime}_{l,l^{\prime}}
as in Definition
4.18
.

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!l^{\prime}!}\int F_{(l^{\prime},l)}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})\prod_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\underline{\widehat{a}}_{+}(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{l}\underline{\widehat{a}}_{-}(\vec{p}_{j})

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\omega_{0}(\vec{p}^{\prime}_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\omega_{0}(\vec{p}_{j})\right)-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\vec{p^{\prime}}_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\vec{p}_{j}\right)\vec{x}\right)d^{3l^{\prime}}\vec{p}^{\prime}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}}d^{3l}\vec{p}_{1\ldots l}.

Alternatively, one may write

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}}\int\mathcal{F}^{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}{\mathpunct{:}}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}_{0,\alpha_{j}}(t,-\alpha_{j}\vec{p}_{j}){\colon}

(4.20)

e^{\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\vec{p}_{j}\right)\vec{x}}d^{3n}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n},

with

\mathcal{F}^{\overbrace{+\ldots+}^{l^{\prime}}\overbrace{-\ldots-}^{l}}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})=\mathcal{F}^{(l^{\prime},l)}(\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})=

F_{(l^{\prime},l)}(-\vec{p^{\prime}}_{1\ldots l^{\prime}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots l})\left(\prod_{j=1}^{l}\sqrt{(2\pi)^{3}2\omega_{0}(\vec{p}_{k})}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}\sqrt{(2\pi)^{3}2\omega_{0}(\vec{p}_{k})}\right),

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}}(\vec{k}_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}})=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots n}),\,\forall\vec{k}_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n},\,\forall\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}^{n},\,\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

We use this form in the formulation of the Feynman rules in Appendix
A
.

Remark 4.8
.

If
F_{(l,l^{\prime})}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right)
, we may further write

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}}\int\mathcal{K}^{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x})_{j=1,\ldots,n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}{\mathpunct{:}}\underline{\widehat{{\phi}}}_{0,\alpha_{j}}(t,\vec{x}_{j}){\colon}d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n},

(4.21)

where
\mathcal{K}^{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
is given by the relation

\mathcal{K}^{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{x}_{1\ldots n})=\int{\mathcal{F}^{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{x}_{1\ldots n})e^{-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\vec{p}_{j}\vec{x}_{j}}}d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}.

The clear advantage of this form is the manifest translational invariance. Unfortunately,
in general case
\mathcal{K}
is only a distribution, so (
4.21
) may have no sense.

Example 4.9
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)
. Then

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=

(4.22)

\int:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}_{0}(t_{j},\vec{x}_{j}):\kappa\left((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x},{t}_{j}-t)_{j=1\ldots n}\right)d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}t_{1\ldots n}

(4.23)

defines an admissible interaction density.

Proof.

Note, that (
4.22
) is clearly well-defined, as it is essentially evaluation of a distribution on a test-function. It involves a non-injective linear transform
(t,t_{1\ldots n})\mapsto((t_{j}-t)_{j=1\ldots n})
, but this is not a problem since we allow
h_{I}
to be a multiplier.

Now we use Example
3.52
to write

h_{I}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\int a_{:\alpha_{1\ldots n}:}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j},\alpha_{j}\vec{p}_{j})e^{\alpha_{j}\mathrm{i}\vec{p}_{j}\vec{x}_{j}}\right)\times

\kappa\left((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x},{t}_{j}-t)_{j=1\ldots n}\right)d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}t_{1\ldots n}d^{3n}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}=

\int a_{:\alpha_{1\ldots n}:}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})e^{\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\vec{p}_{j}\right)\cdot\vec{x}-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\omega_{0}(\vec{p}_{j})\right)t}F_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})d^{3n}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n},

where

F_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})=\int\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j},\alpha_{j}\vec{p}_{j})e^{\alpha_{j}\mathrm{i}\vec{p}_{j}\vec{x}_{j}}\right)\times

\kappa((\vec{x}_{j},{t}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n})d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}t_{1\ldots n}.

Then it is easy to see that
F_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
and we arrive to the form of Remark
4.7
.
∎

Informally, one may say that the admissible interactions are the ones of the form (
4.23
), but with slightly singular
\kappa
. There are two allowed types of singularities: first, we can use the fact that the quantum field restricts with respect to time variable, second we can use the fact that
h_{I}
is a distribution with respect to
\vec{x}
. The first type we have already seen in Remarks
4.7
and
4.8
. As for the second one, we consider the following.

Example 4.10

(Quantum Wick Product)
.

The Quantum Wick Product is a combination of the Quantum Diagonal Map
[
11
,
8
]
, a well-behaved replacement of the pointwise product in the DFR quantum spacetimes
[
25
]
, with the Wick product of quantum field theory. It defines an admissible interaction

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\int\delta\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{x}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((t_{j}-t)^{2})\right)

:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0}(t_{j}|\vec{x}_{j}):\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x})^{2})\right)d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}t_{1\ldots n},

where
l_{P}
is the Planck scale.

Proof.

To see well-definedness let us multiply with
s\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right)
(since the result is in the multipliers space) and act on
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
. After change of variables we get:

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}[f](t)s(t)=\int s(t)\delta\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{y}\right)\times

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((t_{j}-t)^{2})\right)s(t):\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0}(t_{j}|\vec{x}_{j}):\times

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{y}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j})^{2})\right)\underline{f}\left(\vec{y}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j}\right)d^{3n}\vec{x}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}t_{1\ldots n}.

Reading from the right to the left, we see multiplication by

\left((\vec{y},\vec{x}_{1\ldots n})\mapsto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{y}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j})^{2})\right)\right)\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+3}\right),

(augmented) action of
:\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{\phi}}}^{R}_{0}:\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right))
, multiplication by

\left((t,t_{1\ldots n})\mapsto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2l_{P}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}((t_{j}-t)^{2})\right)s(t)\right)\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right),

and application of a numerical distribution to an element of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+3}\right)
.

To see that the interaction is admissible, proceed as in the previous example.
∎

Example 4.11

(Not an example: the star product)
.

Very popular in physics literature (see e.g.
[
4
]
) and initially suggested for the DFR spacetime
[
3
]
, interaction terms, based on the replacement of the pointwise product with a non-commutative product is
not
admissible. Indeed, it would require

\kappa_{*}(\tau_{1\ldots n}\vec{r}_{1\ldots n})\sim\exp\left(-2\mathrm{i}\sum_{i<j}(\tau_{i},\vec{r}_{i})^{\mu}Q_{\mu\nu}(\tau_{j},\vec{r}_{j})^{\nu}\right),

where
(\tau_{i},\vec{r}_{i})^{\mu}
stands for the
\mu
th component of the four-dimensional vector
(\tau_{i},\vec{r}_{i})
,
Q_{\mu\nu}
is the commutator of the coordinates (we use the notation of
[
3
]
) and the Einstein summation rule is assumed. A notable property of
\kappa_{*}
is that it does not decay at large displacements
\vec{r}_{j}
and for this reason can not lead to smooth kernels
F_{l,l^{\prime}}
(note that in the examples above, the interaction kernels are essentialy Fourier transforms of
\kappa
). Physically it means that the interaction does not become negligible at large distances. For this reason the adiabatic cut-off in Definition
4.12
is not enough to regularize the infrared divergences of the theory. In fact, in
[
21
]
it was already noted that the infrared divergences are present already before the adiabatic limit is taken. For rigorous treatment one should introduce further
\vec{r}_{i}
adiabatic cut-off functions already in
\kappa
