. The adiabatic limit of such theories is more involved and is not considered here.

To conclude, we see that Definition
4.4
allows translational-invariant interaction of type (
4.23
) with
\kappa
fast decaying and having restricted singularities.

4.2.3
Admissible Hamiltonian

Definition 4.12
.

Let
h_{I}
be a fixed admissible interaction. Then for each
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
we define the
interaction Hamiltonian

H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}[\Psi]\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
by setting

H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}=g\widehat{{D_{1}}^{*}}\widehat{\circ{\mathfrak{A}_{4}^{1}}}(h_{I}\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{3}})[\Psi\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}],\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}.

Remark 4.13
.

The symbolic form of (
4.12
) is

\underline{H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}}(t)=\int\lambda(t,\vec{x})\underline{h_{I}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)d^{3}\vec{x},t\in\mathbb{R},

where

\lambda(t,\vec{x})=\int{\widetilde{\lambda}(\vec{k},t)e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}d^{3}\vec{x}.

By direct computation

\underline{H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]{\dagger}}}(t)=\underline{H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}}(t)

whenever

\lambda(t,\vec{x})\in\mathbb{R},\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\forall\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Naturally, we interpret
H_{I}
as the interaction representation of the interaction part of the Hamiltonian and
\lambda
as the adiabatic cut-off function.

4.2.4
Evolution and scattering operators

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
h_{I}
be fixed interaction density,
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
and
H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
be the corresponding interaction Hamiltonian (see Definition
4.12
). Then There is unique
U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}{},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right))\llbracket g\rrbracket
such that

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t)=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})},

(4.24)

\partial_{t_{2}}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{1})=-i\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{1}).

(4.25)

The operator
U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
can be found recurrently from

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{1})=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})}-i\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{1})dt,

(4.26)

or explicitly

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{0})=

(4.27)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-i)^{n}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}\cdots\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{i}).

Proof.

To prove the theorem we need only to decipher the symbolic notation of Subsection
2.8
(generalized in Notation
3.23
) and use the Wick Theorem of Remark
3.41
to present the result as a second quantization. For completeness we present all the translations from the symbolic language.

First, we rewrite (
4.26
) explicitly
42
42

42

We use the integration operator
J
defined in Subsection
2.7
.
:

U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)}-i\left(\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{3}^{2}\left(\widehat{J}\circ\widehat{{D_{1}}^{*}}\right)\right)\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,2}^{2,3}H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\otimes_{\bullet}U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]},

(4.28)

where

\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)}\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}^{0}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)),

\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)}(t,t^{\prime})=1,\,\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
H_{I}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
is proportional to
g
, (
4.28
) gives a well-defined expression of higher orders of
U^{g\widetilde{\lambda}}
via its lower orders, and the zeroth order is given by

U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{g=0}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)}.

Thus (
4.28
) can be solved order by order.
By (
2.19
) we see that (
4.24
-
4.26
) is equivalent to (
4.26
).

Finally, we secondary quantize and formally iterate (
4.28
) to get

\widehat{U}^{g\widetilde{\lambda}}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-i)^{n}\left(\left(\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{3}^{2}\left(J\circ\widehat{{D_{1}}^{*}}\right)\right)\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,2}^{2,3}\widehat{H_{I}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\right)^{\circ n}\circ\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)},

which is one of the possible forms of (
4.27
).
∎

Corollary 4.15
.

For
U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
as in proposition above, assuming in addition that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{1}\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{3}\widetilde{\lambda}
is real-valued,

1.

For any
t,t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{0})^{{\dagger}}=\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{0},t);

(4.29)

2.

For any
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
:

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{1})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{1},t_{0})=\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{0})

(4.30)

3.

There are well-defined limits
U_{\mathrm{R}},U_{\mathrm{A}}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}{},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
and
S\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})
defined by

\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)=\lim_{t_{0}\rightarrow-\infty}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{0})

\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{A}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)=\lim_{t_{0}\rightarrow+\infty}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{0},t)

\underline{\widehat{S}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty,t_{0}\rightarrow-\infty}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{0},t);

Moreover, relations (
4.29
-
4.30
) remain correct then one or more of the timestamps goes to
\pm\infty
.

Proof.

For simplicity we use the symbolic notation.

For the first statement follows from (
4.27
) and Remark
3.26
.

For the second statement it is more convenient to use the recurrent relation (
4.26
) and proceed inductively. The zeroth order is trivial. Assuming that (
4.30
) is valid up to
g^{n}
we have

\left(\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{1})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{1},t_{0})\right)_{g^{n}}=

\left(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})}-i\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{1})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{1},t_{0})dt\right)_{g^{n}}=

\left(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})}-i\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t_{0})\right)_{g^{n}}dt=\left(\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{2},t_{0})\right)_{g^{n}},

where we have used the fact that
H_{I}
is proportional to
g
, so we can use the statement (
4.30
) for lower orders.

Finally, the last statement follows from (
2.20
).
∎

Remark 4.16
.

In particular, we see that
\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
,
\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{A}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
,
\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
and
S^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
are valued in the ”unitary” operators in the sense that

\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t^{\prime})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}=\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t^{\prime})=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})}.

From the above we see that
\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{\mathrm{R}}
is an analogue of
U
from the previous subsection. Similarly,
\underline{\widehat{S}}
may be interpreted as the scattering operator.

4.2.5
Interacting fields

To finish the perturbation quantum field theory construction we need to define the interacting quantum field and its momentum similarly to (
4.7
-
4.8
)

Proposition 4.17
.

There are unique operator-valued distributions
\phi^{R},\pi^{R}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
such that

\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{\phi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)^{{\dagger}},

\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{\pi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)^{{\dagger}}.

They satisfy

[\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right),\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}^{\prime}\right)]=0,

[\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right),\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{[\mathrm{R}g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}^{\prime}\right)]=0,

[\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right),\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(\vec{x}^{\prime}|t)]=\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}})},

\partial_{t}\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=-\mathrm{i}[\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t),\underline{\widehat{\phi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)]

\partial_{t}\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=-\mathrm{i}[\underline{\widehat{H_{I}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t),\underline{\widehat{\pi}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)].

Proof.

Direct application of Proposition
4.14
, Corollary
4.15
and translations of the symbolic expressions.∎

For future use, we introduce several alternative forms of the quantum field and its conjugated momentum.

First we define the unrestricted form,

\phi^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}=\mathfrak{I}_{1}^{1}\phi^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}

\pi^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}=\mathfrak{I}_{1}^{1}\pi^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}

to define the unrestricted versions.

Instead of
\phi
and
\pi
it is more convenient to work with

\underline{\widehat{\phi_{\pm}}}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{\phi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0,\pm}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)^{{\dagger}}.

Since
\phi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0,\pm}
is a linear combination of
\phi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0}
and
\pi^{\mathrm{R}}_{0}
, these objects are well-defined already by Proposition
4.17
.

Remark 4.18
.

The decomposition of the interacting field into

\widetilde{\phi}=\widetilde{\phi}_{+}+\widetilde{\phi}_{-}

have no physical sense. Neither do they represent positive and negative energy parts, no they annihilate the vacuum acting from the right and left. It is introduced for the convenience of formulations only.

4.2.6
Correlators

Our next goal is to give a precise form to (
4.12
-
4.17
). We are mostly interested in the Green functions due to their role in physical computations of the scattering amplitudes, but for technical reasons it is more convenient to work with the Wightman function because they can be restricted to fixed values of the timestamps. It is also convenient to work with the correlators of the partial fields
\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}
rather than with the field itself.
So, we start by defining

W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))\llbracket g\rrbracket,

W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\right)=\left(\Omega_{0},S\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{\alpha}\left(t_{j}|\vec{p}_{j}\right)\Omega_{0}\right).

Then we normalize it by introducing

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\right)=\frac{W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\right)}{W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{0}}.

(4.31)

Finally, we define the unrestricted

\mathcal{W}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}=\mathfrak{I}_{1,\ldots,n}^{1,5,\ldots,4n-3}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}

and time-ordered

\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,n})=

(4.32)

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\theta(t_{\sigma_{j}}-t_{\sigma_{j+1}})\right)\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}}\left(t_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}|\vec{p}_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}\right).

versions.

Remark 4.19
.

For completeness, we present a form of (
4.32
) not involving the symbolic notation.

\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,n})=

\sum_{\sigma\in\bm{\Sigma}^{(4)}}L_{\mathfrak{p}^{(\sigma)}_{4}}\circ I_{\mathbb{R}}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{n}^{1}\theta^{\otimes n}\circ L_{K_{n}^{-1}}\circ{{D_{n}}^{*}}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,5,\ldots,4n-3}^{n+1,n+2,\ldots,2n}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}}},

where

K_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}

is

K_{n}(t_{1\ldots n})=\left((t_{j}-t_{j+1)_{j=1\ldots n-1}},t_{n}\right).

Putting it all back together one may verify that

\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\theta(t_{\sigma_{j}}-t_{\sigma_{j+1}})\frac{(\Omega_{0},S\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{\alpha_{\sigma_{j}}}(t_{\sigma_{j}},\vec{p}_{\sigma_{j}})\Omega_{0})}{(\Omega_{0},S\Omega_{0})}=

\frac{\left(\Omega_{0},S{\mathrm{\bf{T}}}\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\phi}^{R}_{\alpha_{\sigma_{j}}}(t_{\sigma_{j}},\vec{p}_{\sigma_{j}})\right\}\Omega_{0}\right)}{(\Omega_{0},S\Omega_{0})}.

The original Wightman and Green functions (
4.14
-
4.15
) are

\mathcal{W}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n})=\sum_{\alpha_{j}\in\{+,-\},j=1,\ldots,n}\mathcal{W}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}).

\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n})=\sum_{\alpha_{j}\in\{+,-\},j=1,\ldots,n}\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((t_{j},\vec{p}_{j})_{j=1\ldots n}).

4.2.7
Summary and outline of the subsection

Comparing Proposition
4.14
, Corollary
4.15
and Proposition
4.17
with the exposition in Subsection
4.2
we conclude that we have constructed the non-local Hamiltonian quantum field theory. So it is natural to fix the following.

Definition 4.20
.

By a massive
admissible Hamiltonian perturbation Quantum Field
(
admissible HpQFT
) Theory we mean a pair
(\omega_{0},h_{I})
, where
\omega_{0}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
is a massive dispersion relation
43
43

43

See Definition
1.2
and
h_{I}\in\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right))
is an admissible interaction density.

All admissible HpQFT are non-local in the physical sense (see Remark
1.1
by construction.
The algebraic locality is broken in general. Still, some residual locality persists as explained in the following three remarks.

Remark 4.21
.

The Hamiltonian can always be presented in the time-localized form (
4.21
). So, from the physical point of view, the interaction is always ”local in time”. In view of this, the main advantage of the Hamiltonian approach with respect to the Lagrangian approaches
44
44

44

Recall that the Lagrangian approaches fail to construct a unitary scattering operator when theory is not local in time
[
4
]
seems to be lost. In fact, as was shown in
[
12
]
, HpQFT is equivalent to a Lagrangian non-local perturbation theory. The corresponding Lagrangian is the Legendre transform of the Hamiltonian with the time-localized interaction (
4.21
)

45
45

45

Note that (
4.21
) can be rewritten in terms of
\phi_{0}
and
\pi_{0}
instead of
\phi_{0\pm}
.
. The Lagrangian theories of this form, however, are more complicated and, in particular, require regularization of the UV divergences despite the spatial non-locality.

Remark 4.22
.

From the algebraic (AQFT) point of view, the commutation relations (
4.1
) make the theory look like a local one if the time is fixed. Unlike the physical locality in the previous remark, this property is inherent to the theory: if the quantum fields
\phi
are related to local measurements in any sense, (
4.1
) has measurable consequences. In particular, it selects a special reference frame, drastically breaking the Lorentz invariance. It is worth noting, that this issue can be bypassed by giving up the direct interpretation of the interacting field. For example, in
[
26
]
it was shown that the Lorentz-covariant approach based on the Yang-Feldman equation can be formulated in the Hamiltonian style by appropriate reference frame-dependent redefinition of the fields.

One may note that these two residual localities are in some sense complementary.

Remark 4.23
.

Looking with attention at (
4.27
) and (
4.30
), one may conclude that all the admissible HpQFT possess some residual causality property, similar to the causality of local theories
[
10
,
19
]
. Formalization of this property is postponed to
[
22
]
where it plays an important role. For now we underline that this property also holds in one particular reference frame only.

Remark 4.24
.

We have assumed that the interaction Hamiltonian has precisely the first order in terms of the interaction constant
g
. However, up to some technical change, all construction remains valid for more general Hamiltonians, presented by formal power series in terms of
g
, provided that the zeroth order vanishes. This is necessary to allow the (finite) counterterms for renormalization. We ignore this detail for simplicity.

Remark 4.25
.

It may look unnatural to assume that the interacting Hamiltonian is fixed already in the interaction picture. In particular, this is the cause of the residual locality observed in Remark
4.21
. In
[
12
]
an alternative way to construct non-local Hamiltonian theories, based on setting by analogy with (
4.10
)

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=\underline{\widehat{U_{\rm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t)\underline{\widehat{h_{int}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)\underline{\widehat{U_{\rm{R}}}}^{{\dagger}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t),

where

\underline{\widehat{h_{int}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=

\int A\left(\tau_{1\ldots n},\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j}}{n}-\vec{x}\right){\mathpunct{:}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\phi}}(t+\tau_{j},\vec{x}_{j}){\mathpunct{:}}\times

B\left(\left(\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x}\right)_{j=1,\ldots,n},\tau_{1\ldots n}\right)d^{3}\vec{x}d^{3n}\vec{r}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}\tau_{1\ldots n},

and
U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
,
\phi
are defined as in Proposition
4.17
. It is easy to see that this operation is well-defined in the sense of formal series
46
46

46

Note that in the leading zeroth with respect to
g
the interacting quantum field can be replaced by the free one, and the higher orders are always expressed via lower ones.
, Propositions
4.14
,
4.17
and Corollary
4.15
still hold, and

\underline{\widehat{h_{I}}}\left(t|\vec{x}\right)=

\int A\left(\tau_{1\ldots n},\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vec{x}_{j}}{n}-\vec{x}\right){\mathpunct{:}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t,t+\tau_{j})\underline{\widehat{\phi}_{0}}(t+\tau_{j},\vec{x}_{j})\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t+\tau_{j},t){\mathpunct{:}}\times

B\left(\left(\vec{x}_{j}-\vec{x}\right)_{j=1,\ldots,n},\tau_{1\ldots n}\right)d^{3}\vec{x}d^{3n}\vec{r}_{1\ldots n}d^{n}\tau_{1\ldots n}.

With minimal technical effort, one may show that the correctly written analogue of the equations of motion (
4.2
) holds. At the same time one may note that the class of effective theories defined in this way is essentially the same.

4.3
Feynman rules with adiabatic cut-off

In this subsection we present a technical version of the Feynman rules for computation of the restricted Wightman function in the presence of the adiabatic cut-off which we use in the proof of Theorem
5.1
. More practical Feynman rules are presented in Appendix. We assume a massive admissible HpQFT to be fixed.

4.3.1
Preparation

We start by the following observation based on construction of
W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
and
U^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}
.

Remark 4.26
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)

W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}g^{V}W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}],

(4.33)

where
W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}]\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+4V}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
is given by

W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},j=0,\ldots,n\\
v_{0}+\ldots+v_{n}=V\end{subarray}}W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}

and
W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+4(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j})}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
is defined by

W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{k}_{1\ldots n},(\tau_{i,j},\vec{q}_{i,j})_{i=0\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)=

J^{(+)_{v_{0}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}J^{(v_{i})}\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)\right)J^{(-)_{v_{n}}}\left(t_{n}|(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}}\right)

(\Omega_{0},\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{h}}}_{I}\left(\tau_{0,j}|\vec{k}_{0,j}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\underline{\widehat{\phi^{R}_{0\alpha_{i}}}}\left(\vec{t}_{i}|\vec{k}_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}\underline{\widehat{\widetilde{h}}}_{I}\left(\tau_{i,j}|\vec{q}_{i,j}\right)\Omega_{0}).

Remark 4.27
.

Here we use the symbolic presentation of the integration operators. For eventual convenience we present the corresponding symbols as discontinuous functions
47
47

47

Here we identify the Heaviside function with the corresponding discontinuous function. This is safe as long as no derivatives are involved (see also Remark
2.33
).

J^{(v)}\left(t,t^{\prime}|\tau_{1\ldots v}\right)=(-1)^{v\mathrm{sign}(t^{\prime}-t)}\theta((\tau_{n}-t)\mathrm{sign}(t^{\prime}-t))\theta((t^{\prime}-\tau_{1})\mathrm{sign}(t^{\prime}-t))\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v-1}\theta(\mathrm{sign}(t^{\prime}-t)(\tau_{j}-\tau_{j+1}))=T_{1,\ldots,v;<}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};-\mathrm{sign}(t^{\prime}-t)}\prod_{j=1}^{v}J\left(t,t^{\prime}|\tau_{j}\right),

where
T_{1,\ldots,v;<}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};+}\in\{0,1\}
is defined below. In the same way, we have

J^{(\pm 1)_{v}}\left(t|\tau_{1\ldots v}\right)=T_{1,\ldots,v;<}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};-1}J_{\pm}\left(t|\tau_{j}\right)

Notation 4.28
.

For any partially ordered set
(A,\prec)
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
, any choice of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{v}\in A
and
\eta=\pm 1
we set the
ordering indicator function

\mathbb{R}^{v}\rightarrow\{0,1\}

(\tau_{1\ldots v})\mapsto T_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{v};\prec}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};+1}.

so that
T_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{v};\prec}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};+1}
if

a_{i}\prec a_{j}\Rightarrow\eta(\tau_{i}-\tau_{j})<0,\,i,j=1,\ldots,v.

(4.34)

The combinatoric interpretation of the factors above leads to the following results:

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
(A^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})
and
(A^{\prime\prime},\prec^{\prime\prime})
be partially ordered sets and define

(A,\prec)=(A^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})\sqcup(A^{\prime\prime},\prec^{\prime\prime}).

Then for any
n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, any
a^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}}\in A^{\prime}
,
a^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}}\in A^{\prime\prime}
,
\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\eta=\pm
one has

T_{a^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}},a^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}};\prec}^{\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}};\eta}=T_{a^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}};\prec^{\prime}}^{\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime}};\eta}T_{a^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}};\prec^{\prime\prime}}^{\tau^{\prime\prime}_{1\ldots n^{\prime\prime}};\eta}

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
(A,\prec)
be a partially ordered set. Let
\overline{\prec}
be the set of all linear extensions of
\prec
. Take r any ,
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{v}\in A
,
\eta=\pm 1
and
\tau_{1\ldots v}
such that

i\neq j\Rightarrow\tau_{i}\neq\tau_{j},\,i,j=1,\ldots,v.

Then

T_{a_{1\ldots v};\prec}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};\eta}=\sum_{\prec^{\prime}\in\overline{\prec}}T_{a_{1\ldots v};\prec^{\prime}}^{\tau_{1\ldots v};\eta}.

Proof of Lemmas
4.29
and
4.30
.

The statements are similar to standard results in combinatorics and elementary probability. In the first case the ordering conditions (
4.34
) for the two disjoint subsets are independent, hence the indicator function factors.

In the second case, we note that if all
\tau_{1\ldots v}
are different, then there is precisely one total
\prec
order satisfying (
4.34
). At the same time, if
\prec
is a partial order, then it satisfies the condition (
4.34
) if and only if one of its linear extensions does. Thus Lemma
4.30
follows by summing over the incompatible possibilities.∎

4.3.2
Feynman rules for unrenormalized correlators

The next step is to apply Wick’s theorem in the form of Proposition
3.35
and Remark
3.41
to get the Feynman rules.

Proposition 4.31
.

The distribution

W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{k}_{1\ldots n},(\tau_{i,j},\vec{q}_{i,j})_{i=0\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)

can be computed by the following Feynman rules
48
48

48

See Subsection
1.1
for the terminology.
.

•

The relevant are the Feynman graphs
\Gamma
with
V=\sum_{j=0}^{n}
enumerated internal vertices
\bullet_{(i,j)}
with
i=0,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,v_{i}
, and
n
enumerated external vertices
\circ_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
;

•

For convenience we fix total order
\prec_{\Gamma}
generated by the following relations:

\circ_{i+1}\prec_{\Gamma}\circ_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}

(4.35)

\bullet_{(i,j)}\prec_{\Gamma}\circ_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}

(4.36)

\circ_{i+1}\prec_{\Gamma}\bullet_{(i,j)},\quad i=1,\ldots n-1,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}

(4.37)

\bullet_{(i+1,j)}\prec_{\Gamma}\bullet_{(i,j)},\quad i=0,\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}-1.

(4.38)

•

To each line assign a free momentum flux
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
directed from the earlier to the later end;

•

The factors corresponding to each element are shown in Table
1
;

•

The overall factor is

C_{\Gamma}=J^{(+)_{v_{0}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}J^{(v_{i})}\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)\right)\times

J^{(-)_{v_{n}}}\left(t_{n}|(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}}\right)=

\left(\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}J_{+}\left(t_{0}|\tau_{0,j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}J\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|\tau_{i,j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}J_{-}\left(t_{n}|\tau_{n,j}\right)\right)\times

T_{1,\ldots,v_{0};<}^{(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}};-}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}T_{1,\ldots,v_{i};<}^{(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}};-\mathrm{sign}(t_{i}-t_{i+1})}\right)T_{1,\ldots,v_{n};<}^{(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}};-}

Proof.

The proof goes as usual with using the Wick Theorem of Proposition
3.35
and Remark
3.41
. Each external vertex is an insertion of
\phi_{0}
, each internal is an insertion of
h_{I}
and each line depicts a contraction as explained in Remark
3.34
and the ordering is just the right-to-left ordering of operators in the composition. The internal vertices are labeled with two numbers
(i,j)

∎

The ordering (
4.35
-
4.38
) completely fixed by labeling of the vertices is convenient for estimations in the next section.

Remark 4.32
.

More standard object would be to deal with symmetrization of
W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
, which can be introduced as a formal variational derivative

g^{-V}\frac{\delta^{W}W^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}{\vec{k}_{1\ldots n}}{t_{1\ldots n}}}{\delta\widetilde{\lambda}(\tau_{1},\vec{q}_{1})\cdots\delta\widetilde{\lambda}(\tau_{V},\vec{q}_{V})}=\left(W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,3n}^{1,\ldots,3n}\bm{\Sigma}^{(4)}\right)\left(t_{1\ldots n}|\vec{k}_{1\ldots n},(\tau_{i},\vec{q}_{i})_{i=1\ldots n}\right).

(4.39)

As
\bm{\Sigma}^{(4)}[\lambda^{\otimes V}]=\lambda^{\otimes V}
, the right-hand sides of (
4.39
) can be safely placed instead of
W^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
in (
4.33
). Similarly we can perform partial symmetrization of

by introducing
W^{\mathrm{R},\Sigma(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+4V}\right)),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
,

W^{\mathrm{R},\Sigma(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}=\left(W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathfrak{A}_{1,\ldots,3n}^{1,\ldots,3n}\bigotimes_{i=0}^{n}\bm{\Sigma}^{(4)}_{v_{i}}\right).

(4.40)

Proposition 4.33
.

W^{\mathrm{R},\Sigma(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots n},\left(t_{1\ldots n}|(\tau_{i,j},\vec{q}_{i,j})_{i=0\ldots n,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)
can be computed according to the following Feynman rules:

Proof.

with respect to permutations of the internal vertices within sets

for each

The factors assigned to elements of the diagrams depend on the relative order of the vertices connected by a line (since the vertex factor distinguishes the incoming and outgoing lines), so we may consider partially ordered graphs as in the statement, but the overall factor will be

\sum_{\prec^{\prime}\mathrm{i}\overline{\prec_{\Gamma}}}C^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\prec^{\prime}}=C^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\prec_{\Gamma}},

where we used notation and statement of Lemma

for the last step.

4.3.3
Normalized correlator

The next step is to identify the denominator of (
4.31
). For it we note that by Corollary
4.15
we have

\left(\Omega_{0},\underline{\widehat{S}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\Omega_{0}\right)=

\left(\Omega_{0},\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{1})\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\underline{\widehat{U}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{j},t_{j}+1)\underline{\widehat{U_{\mathrm{R}}}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}(t_{n})\Omega_{0}\right),\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\forall t_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}.

This leads to the following rather strange version of the Feynman rules.

Proposition 4.34
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}
one has

\left(\Omega_{0},\underline{\widehat{S}}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}\Omega_{0}\right)=\sum_{v^{\prime}_{0\ldots n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}g^{\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j}}N_{n;v^{\prime}_{1\ldots n}}[\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j})}](t_{1\ldots n}),

where
N_{n;v^{\prime}_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j})}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\right)

can be computed according to the Following Feynman rules:

•

The relevant are all partially ordered Feynman graphs
(\Gamma,\prec_{\Gamma})
with
V
internal vertices
\bullet_{(i,j)}
,
i=0,\ldots,n
,

;

The order is constrained by relations

\bullet_{(i+1,j)}\prec\bullet_{(i,j^{\prime})},\quad,i=0,\ldots,n-1,\,j=1,\ldots,v_{i+1},\,j^{\prime}=1,\ldots,v_{i};

•

To each line a momentum flux, directed from earlier to later vertex is assigned;

•

Factors corresponding to elements of the diagrams are presented in Table
1
;

The overall factor is

C^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\prec_{\Gamma}}=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}J_{+}\left(t_{0}|\tau_{0,j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}J\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|\tau_{i,j}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}J_{-}\left(t_{n}|\tau_{n,j}\right))\right)\times

T_{(\bullet_{(0,j)})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}};\prec_{\Gamma}}^{(\tau_{(i,0),i=1,\ldots,dj})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}};+}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}T_{(\bullet_{(i,j)})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}};\prec_{\Gamma}}^{(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}};\mathrm{sign}(t_{i}-t_{i+1})}\right)T_{(\bullet_{(n,j)})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}};\prec_{\Gamma}}^{(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}};-}.

Proof.

Repeat all the constructions of Remark
4.26
and Propositions
4.31
,
4.33
without insertions of
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}
(hence no external vertices in the Feynman graphs).
∎

Corollary 4.35
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
,
v_{0\ldots n}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
define
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+4(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j})}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
as the distributions computed by the Feynman rules of Proposition
4.31
but disregarding all graphs with vacuum components.
Then

\sum_{v^{\prime}_{0\ldots n}}\sum_{v^{\prime\prime}_{0\ldots n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}g^{\sum_{j=0}^{n}(v^{\prime}_{j}+v^{\prime\prime}_{j})}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v^{\prime}_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v^{\prime}_{j})}]\cdot N_{n;v^{\prime\prime}_{0\ldots n}}[\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v^{\prime\prime}_{j})}]=

(4.41)

\sum_{v_{0\ldots n}}g^{\sum_{j=0}^{n}(v_{j})}W^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes(\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j})}],\,\forall f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)

where
\cdot
denotes the pointwise multiplication in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

Proof.

Repeating the argument of Remark
4.32
, we replace
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v^{\prime}_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
with its symmetrization computed by Feynman rules of Proposition
4.33
with the graphs with vacuum corrections omitted. Now substitute that symmetrization together with the diagrammatic expansions of Propositions
4.33
and
4.34
into the left-hand sides of (
4.41
) and expand out the product. Each term of the resulting sum can be bijectively identified with a Feynman graph of Proposition
4.33
. Indeed, the factor coming from
N_{n;v^{\prime\prime}_{0\ldots n}}
corresponds to the vacuum components and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v^{\prime}_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
to the non-vacuum components. The factors, corresponding to the elements of a graph in all the cases are given by Table
1
, so, apart from the overall factors, the contribution of the whole graph is a product of the contributions of the vacuum components and the rest of the graph. To factorize the overall factor we use
Lemma
4.29
and the trivial observation that the vacuum components are always disjoint from the rest of the graph
49
49

49

Recall that the partial orders we consider are always generated by orientation of the edges, so disjoint graph components correspond to disjoint partially ordered subsets.
. Thus the formal sums are equal.
∎

Corollary 4.36
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}g^{V}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}],

where
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}]\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n+4V}\right),\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right))
is given by

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},j=0,\ldots,n\\
v_{0}+\ldots+v_{n}=V\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}},

where
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
are as in Corollary
4.35
.

Notation 4.37
.

The set of all graphs relevant for computation of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n})}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
by Feynman rules of Corollary
4.35
is denoted with

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
. For
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
we denote with
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}
the corresponding contribution.

5
Weak adiabatic limit

5.1
Formulation

In this subsection we formulate the main result of the paper and show how it implies the weak adiabatic limit existence. The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of this statement, presented as a series of technical lemmas.

Basically, we want to claim that the Green function restricts to a smooth function of the momentum and (off-shell) energy defects, and thus can be evaluated when all the defects are zero. There is a small complication caused by singularities far from the origin in the space of energy-momentum defects. To avoid it, we temporally bound support of the adiabatic cut-off function
\widetilde{\lambda}
to remove some unwanted singularities. As we work in the position representation for the time coordinate, it can be done by a convolution.
For
h\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1}\right)
we define

\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4(V+n)}\right))

\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}[f]((\tau,\vec{q})_{1\ldots V},(t,\vec{p})_{1\ldots n})=\int{\prod_{j=1}^{V}h(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots V})f((\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j},\vec{q}_{j})_{j=1\ldots V},(t,\vec{p})_{1\ldots n})}d^{V}\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots V}.

As explained below, in Corollary
5.2
and Remark
5.3
, this trick does not affect the physical adiabatic limit.
We also set

Q_{\Delta}=\{h\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)|\mathrm{supp}{\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{1}}^{-1}[h]\in[-\Delta,\Delta]\}.

Theorem 5.1
.

Fix a massive HpQFT with admissible interaction.
Take
n,V\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}
,
v_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
and
h\in Q_{\frac{M}{V+1}}
.
Then the distribution
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}

restricts to

\mathcal{R}_{3n+1,\ldots,3n+4V}^{1,\ldots,4V}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}\right)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4V},\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)).

Corollary 5.2
.

Fix a massive admissible HpQFT.
Take
\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
such that

\int\widetilde{\lambda}(\vec{k},t)d^{3}{\vec{k}}=1\,\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

and for any
L>0
define
\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
by

\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}(\vec{k},t)=L^{3}\widetilde{\lambda}(\vec{k}L,L^{-1}t).

Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{G}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]=\mathcal{G}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f],

(5.1)

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[g\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]=\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[\alpha]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}{g}[f],

(5.2)

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{W}^{[g\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]=\mathcal{W}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f],

(5.3)

where
\mathcal{G}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}},\mathcal{W}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)\llbracket g\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}[\in]}_{n;g_{1\ldots n}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{(}\right)n))\llbracket g\rrbracket
do not depend on
\widetilde{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Fix
V\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
.
Take
h\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
as in Theorem
5.1
and for each
L>0
define

\widetilde{\lambda}^{(1)}_{L}=\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0}[\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}],

\widetilde{\lambda}^{(2)}_{L}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}-\widetilde{\lambda}^{(1)}_{L}.

It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\lambda}^{(2)}_{L}\subset{L\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right)
, so

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\left[f\otimes\left((\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}_{L}-\widetilde{\lambda}^{(1)\otimes V}_{L}\right)\right]=0.

Now by Theorem
5.1
we have:

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{(1)\otimes V}_{L}](t_{1\ldots n})=(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0})[f\otimes\widetilde{\lambda}^{\otimes V}_{L}]t_{1\ldots n}=

\int{\left(\prod_{j=1}^{V}\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}(\vec{k}_{j},\tau)\right)\mathcal{R}_{3n+1,\ldots,3n+4V}^{1,\ldots,4V}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0})[f]\left(\left(\tau_{j},\vec{k}_{j}\right)_{j=1\ldots V},t_{1\ldots n}\right)d^{3}\vec{k}_{j}d\tau_{j}.}

Considering now
\widetilde{\lambda}
as a distribution and noting that by assumptions of the Corollary it weakly converges to
\delta(\vec{k})
, we get that the limit
L\rightarrow\infty
exists and is equal to

\int{\mathcal{R}_{n+1,\ldots,n+4V}^{1,\ldots,4V}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0})[f]\left(t_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau_{i},\vec{0},\right)_{1\ldots V}\right)d^{V}\tau_{1\ldots V}.}

This proves (
5.2
). Analogously, one can show (
5.1
) and (
5.3
).
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Theorem
5.1
says more than Corollary
5.2
. There is no need to take the uniform scaling limit in all directions. Instead, we can take any sequence or family of function

\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}(t,\vec{k})\underset{L\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\delta(\vec{k})\,\mathrm{in}\,\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right),

provided that

\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}-\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0}\widetilde{\lambda}_{L}\underset{L\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\,\mathrm{in}\,\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}\right).

Extending the proof above we may also conclude the spatial and temporal limits can be taken in any order. Finally, we can put to the adiabatic limit each vertex of the graph individually. As a result, the Feynman rules in the adiabatic limit can be derived from Subsection
4.3
(some variants are listed in Appendix).
This nice behavior differs drastically from the strong adiabatic limit
[
10
]
.

5.2
Applying Feynman rules

n,V\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}
,
v_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
,
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3V}\right)
and
t_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}
by Corollary
4.35
we have:

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f](t_{1\ldots n})=\int J^{(+)_{v_{0}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}}\right)\times

(5.4)

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}J^{(v_{i})}\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)\right)J^{(-)_{v_{n}}}\left(t_{n}|(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}}\right)

F_{\Gamma}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})f\left(\vec{p}_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau_{i,j},\vec{q}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{v_{i}}\Delta_{i,j}^{\Gamma}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\tau_{i,j}\right)\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}d\tau_{i,j}\right)d^{3I_{\Gamma}}\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}}d^{3n}\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the clarification of the notation used in (
5.4
).

For each graph
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
we set
I_{\Gamma}
for the total number of internal (i.e. connecting to internal vertices) lines.
Then
\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3I_{\Gamma}}
denotes the corresponding internal momenta.
\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}
are the
n
external momenta.

For a vertex marked by
(i,j)
of a graph
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n};t_{1\ldots n}}
we denote with
l_{i,j}^{\Gamma}
and
{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}
the number of incoming and outgoing lines respectively.

\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\in\mathbb{R}^{3(l_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma})}

denotes the collection of the corresponding incoming/outgoing momenta as a function of the introduced above internal and external momenta. By construction for
i=0,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,v_{i}
,
\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}
is a linear map. For convenience, we assume that the first
l^{\prime}
and the rest
l
components are the momenta of the outgoing and incoming particles respectively.

Finally for every
\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3V}
and every vertex
(i,j)
(where as always
i=0,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,v
) we define the momentum and energy defects

\vec{q}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}\left(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\right)=\sum_{r=1}^{{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}}\left(\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)_{r}-\sum_{r={l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+1}^{{l}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}}\left(\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)_{r},

\Delta_{i,j}^{\Gamma}\left(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)=

\sum_{r=1}^{{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}}\omega_{0}\left(\left(\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)_{r}\right)-\sum_{r={l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+1}^{{l}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}}\omega_{0}\left(\left(\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right)_{r}\right),

i.e. the sum of the (on-shell) energies (momenta) of all the outgoing particles minus the sum of the (on-shell) energies (momenta) of all the incoming particles. Here by
(\cdot)_{r}
we mean projection on the
r
th factor of
\mathbb{R}^{3({l}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma})}
treated as
\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)^{\otimes({l}_{i,j}^{\Gamma}+{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma})}
.

All vertex factors are collected into one function

F_{\Gamma}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}F_{{l^{\prime}}_{i,j}^{\Gamma},l_{i,j}^{\Gamma,i,j}}\left(\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}(\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}},\vec{p}_{1\ldots n})\right),

\forall\vec{k}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3I_{\Gamma}}\,\forall\vec{p}_{1\ldots n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}.

5.3
Spatial adiabatic limit

We analyze (
5.4
) part by part, starting from the integral over the momenta.

The UV convergence in the momenta space is controlled by the vertex factors as follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Using the notation introduced above, for any graph
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
one has
F_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)}\right)
:

Proof.

Since each
F_{l^{\prime},l}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(l+l^{\prime})}\right)
and
\kappa^{\Gamma}_{i,j}
is a linear function, it is clear that
F_{\Gamma}
is smooth. For the same reason each its factor is at least bounded together with all its partial derivatives considered as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)}
. At the same time each of
\vec{k}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,I_{\Gamma}
and each of
\vec{p}_{j=1,\ldots,n}
appears as an argument of at least one of the factors. From this the standard estimations of type (
2.2
) one concludes that
F_{\Gamma}
, as well as all its partial derivatives, decays at infinity faster than any polynomial.
∎

So, we see that convergence of the integral over the internal momenta in (
5.4
) is completely controlled by the part independent of both the adiabatic cut-off (hidden in the test function
f
) and the timestamps
\tau_{i,j}
.

For the infrared behavior we need also to effectively resolve all momentum conservation constraints expressing the internal momenta via external momenta, momentum defects, and independent loop momenta.
Formally this is expressed by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

For any choice of
n,V\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{0\ldots n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n+1}
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n}v_{i}=V
and a graph
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}
the functionals

\kappa_{i,j}^{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}^{3V}}^{\prime},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,\quad,j=1,\ldots,v_{i}

are linearly independent.

This fact (although not in this form) is well-known in quantum physics, but we still formally prove it for completeness.

Proof.

Assume that there are
\alpha_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i=0,\ldots,n
,
j=1,\ldots,v_{i}
such that

\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{v_{i}}\alpha_{i,j}\kappa_{i,j}^{\Gamma}=0.

(5.5)

For each internal vertex
(i,j)
of the graph define
d_{\Gamma}(i,j)
, the shortest length of a path in
\Gamma
, connecting
(i,j)
with an external vertex. Recall that all connected components of the relevant graphs contain some external vertices, so this function is well-defined. Thus we can prove that
\alpha_{i,j}=0
for any vertex
(i,j)
by induction in
d_{\Gamma}(i,j)
.

•

Base of induction: if
d_{\Gamma}(i,j)=1
then there is an external vertex connected with the internal vertex
(i,j)
.Let it be the
r
th external vertex. We evaluate (
5.5
) by setting all the internal momenta and all the external momenta except
\vec{p}_{r}
to zero. Then only
\kappa_{i,j}^{\Gamma}
survives and we get
\alpha_{i,j}=0
.

•

If we already know that
\alpha_{i,j}=0
for all vertices
(i,j)
such that
d_{\Gamma}(i,j)\leq d_{0}
. Take a vertex
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
such that
d_{\Gamma}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=d_{0}+1
. Then it is connected with a vertex, say,
(i,j)
with
d_{\Gamma}(i,j)=d_{0}
. Assume that one of the connecting edges is marked by
r
. We evaluate (
5.5
) setting all the external and internal momenta except
\vec{k}_{r}
to zero. Then only
\kappa_{i,j}^{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\Gamma}
survive, but we already know that
\alpha_{i,j}=0
. Thus
\alpha_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=0
too.

∎

Corollary 5.6
.

The expression (
5.4
) may be rewritten as

\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f](t_{1\ldots n})=\int J^{(+)_{v_{0}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}J^{(v_{i})}\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)\right)\times

(5.6)

J^{(-)_{v_{n}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}}\right)

F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})\times

f\left(\vec{p}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau_{i,j},\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}\Delta_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\tau_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\right)\times

\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}d\tau_{i,j}d\kappa_{i,j}\right)d^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n-V)}\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}.

Here
\vec{p}_{\Gamma}
is a linear function and
F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}
,
\Delta_{i,j}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}
are precompositions of
F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}
,
\Delta_{i,j}^{\mathrm{S},\Gamma}
with an invertible linear transform.
In particular,
F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(n+I_{\Gamma})}\right)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.5
we can add coordinates
\vec{q}^{\Gamma}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3(n+I_{\Gamma})*}
,
i=1,\ldots,n+I_{\Gamma}-V
completing the family of functionals
\kappa_{i,j}^{\Gamma}
to the basis. Then the form (
5.6
) exists. The last statement is a reformulation of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

5.4
Temporal adiabatic limit

Now we focus on integrals over the timestamp positions, starting from the inner vertices.
From (
5.4
) we get

\left(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(v_{1\ldots n});\Gamma}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}\right)[f](t_{1\ldots n})=

(5.7)

\int J^{(+)_{v_{0}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{0,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{0}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}J^{(v_{i})}\left(t_{i+1},t_{i}|(\tau_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{i}}\right)\right)\times

J^{(-)_{v_{n}}}\left(t_{1}|(\tau_{n,j})_{j=1,\ldots,v_{n}}\right)

F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})\times\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}h(\tau_{i,j}-\tau^{\prime}_{i,j})

f\left(\vec{p}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j},\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}\Delta_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\tau_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\right)\times

\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}d\tau_{i,j}d\tau^{\prime}_{i,j}d\kappa_{i,j}\right)d^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n-V)}\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}.

Since all the integrals above are absolutely convergent, the integration can be performed in any order. This way we arrive to

\left(\mathcal{W}^{(v_{0\ldots n})}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}\right)[f](t_{1\ldots n})=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}}\int

(5.8)

F^{\mathrm{S}}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})\times

O_{[h]\Gamma,0}\left(\left(\tau^{\prime}_{0,j}\right)_{j=1\ldots v_{0}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t_{1}\right)

\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n-1}O_{[h]\Gamma,i^{\prime}}\left(\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j}\right)_{j=1\ldots v_{i^{\prime}}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t_{i},t_{i+1}\right)\times

O_{[h]\Gamma,n}\left(\left(\tau^{\prime}_{,n}\right)_{j=1\ldots v_{n}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t_{n}\right)

f\left(\vec{p}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j},\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}d\tau^{\prime}_{i,j}d\kappa_{i,j}\right)d^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n-V)}\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},

where for
i^{\prime}=1,\ldots,n-1

O_{[h]\Gamma,i^{\prime}}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{i^{\prime}}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t^{\prime},t\right)=

(5.9)

\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}d\tau_{1}\int_{t}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{\tau_{v_{i}-1}}d\tau_{v_{i}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}\Delta_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\tau_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}h(\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}d\tau_{j},

and

O_{[h]\Gamma,0}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{0}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t^{\prime},t\right)=

(5.10)

\int_{t}^{+\infty}d\tau_{1}\int_{t}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{\tau_{v_{0}-1}}d\tau_{v_{0}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{0,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\tau_{0,j^{\prime}}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}h(\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}d\tau_{j},

O_{[h]\Gamma,n}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{n}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t\right)=

(5.11)

\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\cdots\int_{-\infty}^{\tau_{v_{n}-1}}d\tau_{v_{n}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{n}}\Delta_{n,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\tau_{n,j^{\prime}}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}h(\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}d\tau_{j}.

Note that here we use the direct functional interpretation of distributions
J^{(n)}
and
J^{(n)_{\pm}}
rather than the combinatoric version of Remark
4.27
. The main advantage of this approach is the manifest smoothness with respect to
t_{1\ldots n}
.

There are two very different kinds of contributions: the
inner vertices

\bullet_{(i,j)}
,
i=1,\ldots,n_{1}
, and
outer vertices

\bullet_{(0,j)}
and
\bullet_{(n,j)}
. We treat them separately in two subsections. The terminology comes from the fact that the inner vertices correspond to operators standing between two source insertions (the external vertices), while the operators corresponding to the outer ones appear either before or after all the insertions. This distinction is crucial for the treatment of the corresponding factors.

5.4.1
Inner vertices: compact region of integration

In (
5.9
) the region of integration is compact and all the estimations are rather direct. We have to deal with the fact that the integration region depends on the arguments but they do not bring anything but a polynomial factor. It is convenient to first formalize this fact.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix
v,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m+v+2}\right)
. Define a function
H
on
\mathbb{R}^{m+v+2}
by

H(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v},x,t^{\prime},t)=\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}d\tau_{1}\int_{t}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\cdots\int_{t}^{\tau_{v-1}}d\tau_{v}g((\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j})_{j=1\ldots v},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t)d^{v}\tau_{1\ldots v},

\forall t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\forall\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v}\in\mathbb{R}^{v},\,\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

Then
H\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+v+2}\right)
.

Proof.

The smoothness is clear. In particular,
H
is continuous together with all its derivatives, so it is enough to estimate it for
t\neq t^{\prime}
. In this case we can change variables of integration to

\xi_{i}=\frac{\tau_{i}-t}{t^{\prime}-t},\quad,i=1,\ldots,v.

This way for any allowed arguments we get

H(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t)=

(t^{\prime}-t)^{v}\int_{0}^{1}d\xi_{1}\int_{0}^{\xi_{1}}d\xi_{2}\ldots\int_{0}^{\xi_{n-1}}d\xi_{n}g((\xi_{j}(t^{\prime}-t)+t-\tau^{\prime}_{j})_{j=1\ldots v},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t)d^{v}\xi_{1\ldots v}.

The integrand is clearly fast decaying with respect to
t
,
t^{\prime}
and
\bm{x}
. For
\tau^{\prime}_{j}
note, that
whenever
0\leq\xi_{j}\leq 1
, we have

(1+|\xi_{j}(t^{\prime}-t)+t-\tau^{\prime}_{j}|)(1+|t|)(1+|t^{\prime}|)\geq 1+|\tau^{\prime}_{j}|,

so the integrand is fast decaying with respect to
\tau^{\prime}
. Then using standard estimations of type (
2.3
-
2.2
) we get that the integral is fast decaying, and the partial derivatives can be treated in the same way.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

For
n,V,v_{1\ldots n},\alpha_{1\ldots n},\Gamma
as in Theorem
5.1
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
h\in Q_{\frac{M}{V+1}}
one has

O_{[h]\Gamma,i}\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{v_{i}},\mathbb{R}^{3(I^{\Gamma}+n)+2}\right).

Proof.

By Remark
2.15
it is enough to prove that for any
\chi\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+2}\right)

\mathfrak{M}_{\chi}^{(v)}[O_{[h]\Gamma,i^{\prime}}]\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+2+v_{i}}\right)
.

For that use Lemma
5.7
with
v=v_{i}
,
m=3(I_{\Gamma}+n)
and

g(\tau_{1\ldots v_{i}},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t)=

\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}\Delta_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\bm{x}\right)\tau_{j^{\prime}}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}h(\tau_{j})\chi\left(\bm{x},t^{\prime},t\right),\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+2}.

Then

\mathfrak{M}_{\chi}^{(v)}[O_{[h]\Gamma,i^{\prime}}]\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{i}},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t\right)=H(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{i}},\bm{x},t^{\prime},t)R(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{i}},\bm{x})

where
H\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{v_{i}+3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+2}\right)
is constructed in Lemma
5.7
and

R(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{i}},\bm{x})=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{v_{i^{\prime}}}\Delta_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}\left(\bm{x}\right)\tau^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\right).

The statement then follows by
R\in\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{v_{i}+3(I_{\Gamma}+n)}\right)
.
∎

5.4.2
Outer vertices: spectral properties

The integration regions in the right-hand sides of (
5.10
-
5.11
) are not compact, so at the first sight, the convergence is controlled by the adiabatic cut-off function only. The situation becomes more clear in the spectral representation. Then
h\in Q_{\frac{M}{V+1}}
makes the integration region compact. The key observation is that physically these groups of vertices describe creation of several particles from the vacuum (if
i=n
) or completion annihilation of particles up to the vacuum (if
i=0
) as an on-shell process.

We consider in details the case
i=0
.

It is convenient to shift the variables of integration to
s^{\mathrm{f}}_{1\ldots v}
as follows

\tau_{j}=t+\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j}s^{\mathrm{f}}_{j^{\prime}}.

Then the integration region becomes
\mathbb{R}^{v_{0}}_{+}
and

O_{[h]\Gamma,0}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{0}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t\right)=

(5.12)

\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\Omega_{v_{0}}^{\mathrm{f},\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)t\right)\times

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{v_{0}}_{+}}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{0}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)s_{j}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}h\left(t_{0}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j}s_{j^{\prime}}-\tau^{\prime}_{j}\right)ds_{j}.

Here we introduced the commulated energy deffects

\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}=\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{v_{0}}(-\Delta_{0,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}).

(5.13)

The following observation is important.

Remark 5.9
.

For any values of the arguments

\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\geq M.

(5.14)

Proof.

It follows from the fact that all lines started at some of vertices
(0,j)
with
j=1,\ldots,v^{\prime}
must end at some other vertex from the same set, because the outgoing state is the vacuum and all external vertices may appear only before
(0,j)
. Let us see some details.

Each
(-\Delta_{0,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma})
contains both positive contributions of the incoming lines and negative contributions of the outgoing lines of the vertex
(0,j^{\prime})
.
Each outgoing line should end at some later vertex
(0,j^{\prime\prime})
,
j^{\prime\prime}>j
. In other words, all negative contributions to the right-hand sides of (
5.13
]) cancel out. Finally, if for some vertex all positive contributions were also canceled it would mean that this vertex belongs to a disconnected pieces of the diagram which is forbidden. Then by the mass gap assumed we get (
5.14
).
∎

Let us see how it helps to deal with
O_{[h]\Gamma,0}
. As long as
h\in Q_{\frac{M}{V+1}}
, (
5.12
) is equivalent to

O_{[h]\Gamma,0}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{0}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t\right)=

(5.15)

\int\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j}+\Omega_{v_{0}}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right)t\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{i}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\tau^{\prime}_{j}\Delta_{j}\right)\widetilde{h}(\Delta_{j})d\Delta_{j}}{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)+\mathrm{i}0}.

Remark 5.10
.

In principle, this expression involves symbolic integral which should be understood as an evaluation of a distribution

F(\Delta_{1\ldots v_{0}})=\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{i}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\tau^{\prime}_{j}\Delta_{j}\right)\widetilde{h}(\Delta_{j})}{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)+\mathrm{i}0}

on a test function. But by Remark
5.9
and
h\in Q_{\frac{M}{V+1}}
the denominators are bounded from below,

\left|-\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{0}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right|\geq

(5.16)

M-v_{0}\frac{M}{V+1}\geq\frac{M}{V+1}.

So, the integral in (
5.15
) may be treated literally and
+\mathrm{i}0
can be ignored.

In the same way we get

O_{[h]\Gamma,n}\left(\tau^{\prime}_{1\ldots v_{n}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t^{\prime}\right)=

(5.17)

\int\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{v_{n}}\Delta_{j}-\Omega_{v_{n}}^{\mathrm{f},\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right)t^{\prime}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{i}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\tau^{\prime}_{j}\Delta_{j}\right)\widetilde{h}(\Delta_{j})d\Delta_{j}}{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j^{\prime}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{i}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)+\mathrm{i}0},

where

\Omega_{i}^{\mathrm{i}\Gamma}=\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j}(\Delta_{0,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{S}\Gamma}).

(5.18)

Similarly to Remark
5.9
we have

\left|\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j^{\prime}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{i}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right|\geq\frac{M}{V+1},

(5.19)

and in particular the
+\mathrm{i}0
symbol can be ignored.

Lemma 5.11
.

For For
n,V,v_{1\ldots n},\alpha_{1\ldots n},\Gamma
as in Theorem
5.1
,

O_{[h]\Gamma,i}\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{v_{i}},\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+1}\right),\,i=0,n.

Proof.

By (
5.15
) and (
5.17
) for
i=0,n

O_{[h]\Gamma,i}
is a Fourier transform of
\widetilde{O}_{[h]\Gamma,i}
,

\widetilde{O}_{[h]\Gamma,0}\left(\Delta_{1\ldots v_{0}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t\right)=

\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j}+\Omega_{v_{0}}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right)t\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{h}(\Delta_{j})}{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{v_{0}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{f}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)+\mathrm{i}0}

and

\widetilde{O}_{[h]\Gamma,n}\left(\Delta_{1\ldots v_{n}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t^{\prime}\right)=

\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{v_{n}}\Delta_{j}-\Omega_{v_{n}}^{\mathrm{f},\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)\right)t^{\prime}\right)\times

\prod_{j=1}^{v_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{h}(\Delta_{j})}{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j^{\prime}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}-\Omega_{j}^{\mathrm{i}\Gamma}\left(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V}\right)+\mathrm{i}0},

where we used the accumulated energy defects (
5.13
) and
(
5.18
). Taking into account (
5.16
) and (
5.19
) we get

\widetilde{O}_{[h]\Gamma,i}\in\mathcal{SO_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{v_{i}},\mathbb{R}^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n)+1}\right),\,i=0,n.

Thus the same holds for its Fourier transform.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

It is worth noting that the argument above is applicable only because both initial and final states are the vacuum. In particular, it does not work for the adiabatic limit of scattering amplitudes.

5.5
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Proof.

We have only to sum up the results of the two previous subsections.
Indeed, applying Corollary
5.8
and Lemma
5.11
to the form (
5.8
), we have

\left(\mathcal{W}^{(v_{0\ldots n})}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{V,n}\right)[f](t_{1\ldots n})=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}}\int

N^{[h]}_{\Gamma}(\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j}\right)_{i=0\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t_{1\ldots n})\times

f\left(\vec{p}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j},\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{v_{i}}d\tau^{\prime}_{i,j}d\kappa_{i,j}\right)d^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n-V)}\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},

where

N^{[h]}_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{O_{M}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{V+3(I_{\Gamma}+n)},\mathcal{T}_{n}\right),

and
\vec{p}_{\Gamma}
is a linear function from Lemma
5.5
.
Then the necessary restriction is defined by

\mathcal{R}_{n+1,\ldots,n+4V}^{1,\ldots,4V}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{R}(V)}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{[h]}^{1,0})[f]((\tau^{\prime}_{i,j},\kappa_{i,j})_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},t_{1\ldots n})=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha_{1\ldots n}}^{v_{0\ldots n}}}\int

N^{[h]}_{\Gamma}(\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j}\right)_{i=0\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},t_{1\ldots n})\times

f\left(\vec{p}_{\Gamma}(\left(\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{i}},\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V})_{1\ldots n},\left(\tau^{\prime}_{i,j},\vec{\kappa}_{i,j}\right)_{i=1\ldots n,j=1\ldots v_{n}}\right)\times

d^{3(I_{\Gamma}+n-V)}\vec{q}_{1\ldots I_{\Gamma}+n-V},

∎

\mathcal{F}

\phi^{n}

\mathcal{F}

[

Remark A.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}
and a formal parameter
g
the symbolic expression for

\mathcal{G}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]
may be computed by the following Feynman rules:

•

The relevant are all partially ordered Feynman graphs
(\Gamma,\prec_{\Gamma})
with
V
non enumerated internal vertices
\bullet
,
j=1,\ldots,v
and
n
external vertices
\circ_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
and no vacuum corrections;

•

To each line a momentum flux, directed from earlier to later vertex is assigned;

•

To each internal vertex is assigned a timestamp (a variable in
\mathbb{R}
);

•

For convenience with the external vertex
\circ_{i}
we associate the timestamp
t_{i}
and the momentum
\vec{p}_{i}
which are treated as free variables;

•

Factors corresponding to elements of the diagrams are presented in Table
2
;

•

The overall factor is
f((t_{i},\vec{k}_{i})_{i=1\ldots n})
;

•

The integration region is bounded by the condition that timestamps corresponding to earlier vertices (internal or external) have smaller values than the ones corresponding to the later.

For practice it is often more convenient to work with energy and momentum presentation rather than with the mixed one as above. We define

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

by

\mathcal{G}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((\vec{p}_{i},t_{i})_{i=1\ldots n})=\int\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}((\vec{p}_{i},\omega_{i})_{i=1\ldots n})e^{-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}t_{i}}d^{n}\omega_{1\ldots n}.

Then it is convenient to compute the integrals over the timestamps of both internal and external timestamps similarly to treatment of the outer vertices in Subsubsection
5.4
following result. For that we should use total rather than partial order.

Remark A.2
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4n}\right)

\alpha_{1\ldots n}\in\{+,-\}
and a formal parameter
g
the symbolic expression for

\mathcal{G}^{[g]}_{n;\alpha_{1\ldots n}}[f]
may be computed by the following Feynman rules:

•

The relevant are all totally ordered Feynman graphs
(\Gamma,\prec_{\Gamma})
with
V
non enumerated internal vertices
\bullet
,
j=1,\ldots,v
and
n
external vertices
\circ_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
and no vacuum corrections;

•

To each line a momentum flux and energy, directed from the earlier to the later vertex is assigned;

•

For convenience with the external vertex
\circ_{i}
we associate the energy
\omega_{i}
and the momentum
\vec{p}_{i}
which are treated as free variables;

•

Factors corresponding to elements of the diagrams are presented in Table
3
;

•

The overall factor is
f((\omega_{i},\vec{k}_{i})_{i=1\ldots n})\frac{\delta(\sum_{\diamond}\Omega_{\diamond})}{\prod^{\prime}_{\diamond}\sum_{\diamond^{\prime}\prec\diamond}\Omega_{\diamond^{\prime}}}
, where the product goes over all (internal and external) vertices
\diamond
except for the last one, sum goes over all vertices
\diamond^{\prime}
preceding
\diamond
and the on-shell energy defects are defined as

\Omega_{\diamond}
are computed according to Table
A.3
;

•

Alternatively the vertex factor can be replaced by

f((\omega_{i},\vec{k}_{i})_{i=1\ldots n})\frac{\delta(\sum_{\diamond}\Omega_{\diamond})}{\prod^{\prime}_{\diamond}\sum_{-\diamond^{\prime}\succ\diamond}\Omega_{\diamond^{\prime}}+i0}
, where this time the product goes over all (internal and external) vertices except for the first one;

The two versions of the Feynman rules are quite effective for computation of the contribution of a small graph. But they are too much different from the traditional formulation in the physical literature which makes even qualitative comparison with the local theories more difficult. In addition to that the standard techniques to manage large classes of Feynman graphs, such as resummations of the one-particle corrections, are not available for the formulation of Remark
A.2
where the total ordering makes all the pieces entangled.

To avoid that we replace summations over ordering with summations over orientations of lines. Moreover, we formally allow lines that are incoming or outgoing at both ends. For that we modify the propagator so that it vanishes whenever the order of timestamps and orientations of the lines are not consistent. Note that in this formulation the direction of the energy and momentum flows is not fixed anymore.

.

Remark A.4

One may note that in the local case

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}}(\vec{k}_{1},\ldots,\vec{k}_{m})=\lambda_{m}

summation over the time-orientation of the propagators may be performed leading to the standard Feynman propagator.

We refer to
[
12
]
where the Feynman rules of Remark
A.4
were used to make contact of the time-dependent Hamiltonian perturbation theory with the time-independent one used in
[
27
]
to compute the quantum corrections to dispersion relations. It was shown, that as in the conventional QFT the dispersion relation corrections appear as shifts of the pole of the corrected propagator. We postpone the precise interpretation of this result to further publications.
